And she is painted also with a [[Wheel\|wheel]], to signify to you—which is the moral of it—that she is turning and inconstant and [[Mutability\|mutability]] and variation. And her foot, look you, is fixed upon a spherical stone, which rolls and rolls and rolls. In good [[Truth\|truth]], the poet makes a most excellent description of it; Fortune is an excellent moral. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Fortune is Bardolph’s foe and frowns on him,<br>For he hath stol’n a pax, and hangèd must a be.<br>A damned [[Death\|death]]—<br>Let gallows gape for dog, let man go free,<br>And let not hemp his windpipe suffocate.<br>But Exeter hath given the doom of [[Death\|death]]<br>For pax of little price.<br>Therefore go speak, the Duke will hear thy voice,<br>And let not Bardolph’s vital thread be cut<br>With edge of penny cord and vile reproach.<br>Speak, captain, for his life, and I will thee requite. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Ensign [[Pistol]], I do partly understand your meaning. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Why then rejoice therefor. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Certainly, ensign, it is not a thing to rejoice at. For if, look you, he were my brother, I would [[Desire\|desire]] the Duke to use his good pleasure, and put him to executions. For discipline ought to be used. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Die and be damned! and fico for thy [[Friendship\|friendship]]. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | It is [[Well]]. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | The fig of Spain. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Very good. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | I say the fig within thy bowels and thy dirty maw. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Captain [[Gower]], cannot you hear it lighten and thunder? |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Why, is this the ensign you told me of? I remember him now. A bawd, a cutpurse. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | I’ll assure you, a uttered as prave words at the pridge as you shall see in a [[Summer\|summer]]’s day. But it is very [[Well\|well]]. What he has spoke to me, that is [[Well\|well]], I warrant you, when [[Time\|time]] is serve. |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Why ’tis a gull, a [[Fool\|fool]], a rogue, that now and then goes to the wars, to grace himself at his return into [[London]] under the form of a soldier. And such fellows are perfect in the great commanders’ names, and they will learn you by rote where services were done—at such and such a sconce, at such a breach, at such a convoy, who came off bravely, who was shot, who disgraced, what terms the enemy stood on—and this they con perfectly in the phrase of war, which they trick up with new-tuned oaths. And what a beard of the General’s cut and a horrid suit of the camp will do among foaming bottles and ale-washed wits is wonderful to be thought on. But you must learn to know such slanders of the age, or else you may be marvellously mistook. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | I tell you what, Captain [[Gower]], I do perceive he is not the man that he would gladly make show to the world he is. If I find a hole in his coat, I will tell him my mind.<br>A drum is heard<br>Hark you, the [[King]] is coming, and I must speak with him from the pridge. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Harry and his poor soldiers, with drum and colours |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | God pless your majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | How now, [[Fluellen]], com’st thou from the bridge? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Ay, so please your majesty. The [[Duke of Exeter]] has very gallantly maintained the pridge. The French is gone off, look you, and there is gallant and most prave passages. Marry, th’athversary was have possession of the pridge, but he is enforced to retire, and the [[Duke of Exeter]] is master of the pridge. I can tell your majesty, the Duke is a prave man. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | What men have you lost, [[Fluellen]]? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | The perdition of th’athversary hath been very great, reasonable great. Marry, for my part I think the Duke hath lost never a man, but one that is like to be executed for robbing a church, one Bardolph, if your majesty know the man. His face is all bubuncles and whelks and knobs and flames o’ [[Fire\|fire]], and his lips blows at his nose, and it is like a coal of [[Fire\|fire]], sometimes plue and sometimes red. But his nose is executed, and his [[Fire\|fire]]’s out. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | We would have all such offenders so cut off, and we here give express charge that in our marches through the country there be nothing compelled from the villages, nothing taken but paid for, none of the French upbraided or abused in disdainful language. For when lenity and cruelty play for a kingdom, the gentler gamester is the soonest winner. Tucket. Enter Montjoy |
| MONTJOY | You know me by my habit. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | [[Well]] then, I know thee. What shall I know of thee? |
| MONTJOY | My master’s mind. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Unfold it. |
| MONTJOY | Thus says my [[King]]: ‘Say thou to Harry of England, though we seemed dead, we did but [[Sleep\|sleep]]. Advantage is a better soldier than rashness. Tell him, we could have rebuked him at Harfleur, but that we thought not good to bruise an injury till it were full ripe. Now we speak upon our cue, and our voice is imperial. England shall repent his folly, see his weakness, and admire our sufferance. Bid him therefore consider of his ransom, which must proportion the losses we have borne, the subjects we have lost, the disgrace we have digested—which in weight to re-answer, his pettiness would bow under. For our losses, his exchequer is too poor; for th’ffusion of our [[Blood\|blood]], the muster of his kingdom too faint a number; and for our disgrace, his own person kneeling at our feet but a weak and worthless satisfaction. To this add defiance, and tell him for conclusion he hath betrayed his followers, whose condemnation is pronounced.’ So far my [[King]] and master; so much my office. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | What is thy name? I know thy quality. |
| MONTJOY | Montjoy. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Thou dost thy office fairly. Turn thee back<br>And tell thy [[King\|king]] I do not seek him now,<br>But could be willing to march on to Calais<br>Without impeachment, for to say the sooth—<br>Though ’tis no wisdom to confess so much<br>Unto an enemy of craft and vantage—<br>My people are with sickness much enfeebled,<br>My numbers lessened, and those few I have<br>Almost no better than so many French;<br>Who when they were in health—I tell thee herald,<br>I thought upon one pair of English legs<br>Did march three Frenchmen. Yet forgive me, God,<br>That I do brag thus. This your air of France<br>Hath blown that vice in me. I must repent.<br>Go, therefore, tell thy master here I am;<br>My ransom is this frail and worthless trunk,<br>My army but a weak and sickly guard.<br>Yet, God before, tell him we will come on,<br>Though France himself and such another neighbour<br>Stand in our way. There’s for thy labour, Montjoy.<br>Go bid thy master [[Well\|well]] advise himself.<br>If we may pass, we will; if we be hindered,<br>We shall your tawny ground with your red [[Blood\|blood]]<br>Discolour. And so, Montjoy, fare you [[Well\|well]].<br>The sum of all our answer is but this:<br>We would not seek a battle as we are,<br>Nor as we are we say we will not shun it.<br>So tell your master. |
| MONTJOY | I shall deliver so. Thanks to your highness. Exit |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I [[Hope\|hope]] they will not come upon us now. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | We are in God’s hand, brother, not in theirs.<br>March to the bridge. It now draws toward [[Night\|night]].<br>Beyond the [[River\|river]] we’ll encamp ourselves,<br>And on tomorrow bid them march away. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Constable, Lord Rambures, the Dukes of Orléans and Bourbon, with others |
| CONSTABLE | Tut, I have the best armour of the world. Would it were day. |
| ORLEANS | You have an excellent armour. But let my [[Horse\|horse]] have his due. |
| CONSTABLE | It is the best [[Horse\|horse]] of Europe.<br>ORLÉANS Will it never be morning? |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON My lord of Orléans and my Lord High Constable, you talk of [[Horse\|horse]] and armour? |
| CONSTABLE | ORLÉANS You are as [[Well\|well]] provided of both as any prince in the world. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON What a long [[Night\|night]] is this! I will not change my [[Horse\|horse]] with any that treads but on four pasterns. Ah ha! He bounds from the earth as if his entrails were hares-le cheval volant, the Pegasus, qui a les narines de feu! When I bestride him, I soar, I am a hawk; he trots the air, the earth sings when he touches it, the basest horn of his hoof is more musical than the pipe of Hermes. |
| CONSTABLE | ORLÉANS He’s of the colour of the nutmeg. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON And of the heat of the ginger. It is a beast for Perseus. He is pure air and [[Fire\|fire]], and the dull elements of earth and [[Water\|water]] never appear in him, but only in patient stillness while his rider mounts him. He is indeed a [[Horse\|horse]], and all other jades you may call beasts. |
| CONSTABLE | Indeed, my lord, it is a most absolute and excellent [[Horse\|horse]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON It is the prince of palfreys. His neigh is like the bidding of a monarch, and his countenance enforces homage. |
| CONSTABLE | ORLÉANS No more, cousin. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Nay, the man hath no wit, that cannot from the rising of the lark to the lodging of the [[Lamb\|lamb]] vary deserved praise on my palfrey. It is a theme as fluent as the sea. Turn the sands into eloquent tongues, and my [[Horse\|horse]] is argument for them all. ‘Tis a subject for a sovereign to reason on, and for a sovereign’s sovereign to ride on, and for the world, familiar to us and unknown, to lay apart their particular functions, and [[Wonder\|wonder]] at him. I once writ a [[Sonnet\|sonnet]] in his praise, and began thus: ‘[[Wonder]] of [[Nature\|nature]]!—’ |
| CONSTABLE | ORLÉANS I have heard a [[Sonnet\|sonnet]] begin so to one’s mistress. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Then did they imitate that which I composed to my courser, for my [[Horse\|horse]] is my mistress. |
| CONSTABLE | ORLÉANS Your mistress bears [[Well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Me [[Well\|well]], which is the prescribed praise and perfection of a good and particular mistress. |
| CONSTABLE | Nay, for methought yesterday your mistress shrewdly shook your back. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON So perhaps did yours. |
| CONSTABLE | Mine was not bridled. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON O then belike she was old and gentle, and you rode like a kern of Ireland, your French hose off, and in your strait strossers. |
| CONSTABLE | You have good judgement in horsemanship. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Be warned by me then: they that ride so, and ride not warily, fall into foul bogs. I had rather have my [[Horse\|horse]] to my mistress. |
| CONSTABLE | I had as lief have my mistress a jade. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON I tell thee, Constable, my mistress wears his own hair. |
| CONSTABLE | I could make as true a boast as that, if I had a sow to my mistress. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON ‛Le chien est retourné à son propre vomissement, et la truie lavée au bourbier.’ Thou makest use of anything. |
| CONSTABLE | Yet do I not use my [[Horse\|horse]] for my mistress, or any such proverb so little kin to the purpose. |
| RAMBURES | My Lord Constable, the armour that I saw in your tent tonight, are those [[Stars\|stars]] or suns upon it? |
| CONSTABLE | [[Stars]], my lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Some of them will fall tomorrow, I [[Hope\|hope]]. |
| CONSTABLE | And yet my sky shall not want. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON That may be, for you bear a many superfluously, and ’twere more [[Honour\|honour]] some were away. |
| CONSTABLE | Even as your [[Horse\|horse]] bears your praises, who would trot as [[Well\|well]] were some of your brags dismounted. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Would I were able to load him with his [[Desert\|desert]]! Will it never be day? I will trot tomorrow a mile, and my way shall be paved with English faces. |
| CONSTABLE | I will not say so, for [[Fear\|fear]] I should be faced out of my way. But I would it were morning, for I would fain be about the ears of the English. |
| RAMBURES | Who will go to hazard with me for twenty prisoners? |
| CONSTABLE | You must first go yourself to hazard, ere you have them. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON ’Tis midnight. I’ll go arm myself. Exit |
| CONSTABLE | ORLÉANS The Duke of Bourbon longs for morning. |
| RAMBURES | He longs to eat the English. |
| CONSTABLE | I think he will eat all he kills.<br>ORLÉANS By the white hand of my lady, he’s a gallant prince. |
| CONSTABLE | Swear by her foot, that she may tread out the oath.<br>ORLÉANS He is simply the most active gentleman of France. |
| CONSTABLE | Doing is activity, and he will still be doing.<br>ORLÉANS He never did harm that I heard of. |
| CONSTABLE | Nor will do none tomorrow. He will keep that good name still.<br>ORLÉANS I know him to be valiant. |
| CONSTABLE | I was told that by one that knows him better than you.<br>ORLÉANS What’s he? |
| CONSTABLE | Marry, he told me so himself, and he said he cared not who knew it.<br>ORLÉANS He needs not; it is no hidden virtue in him. |
| CONSTABLE | By my [[Faith\|faith]], sir, but it is. Never anybody saw it but his lackey. ’Tis a hooded valour, and when it appears it will bate.<br>ORLÉANS ‘Ill will never said [[Well\|well]].’ no |
| CONSTABLE | I will cap that proverb with ‘There is flattery in [[Friendship\|friendship]].’<br>ORLÉANS And I will take up that with ‘Give the devil his due.’ |
| CONSTABLE | [[Well]] placed! There stands your friend for the devil. Have at the very eye of that proverb with ‘A pox of the devil!’<br>ORLÉANS You are the better at proverbs by how much ‘a [[Fool\|fool]]’s bolt is soon shot’. |
| CONSTABLE | You have shot over.<br>ORLÉANS ’Tis not the first [[Time\|time]] you were overshot. Enter a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | My Lord High Constable, the English lie within fifteen hundred paces of your tents. |
| CONSTABLE | Who hath measured the ground? |
| MESSENGER | The Lord Grandpré. |
| CONSTABLE | A valiant and most expert gentleman. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Messenger |
| CONSTABLE | Would it were day! Alas, poor Harry of England. He<br>longs not for the dawning as we do.<br>ORLÉANS What a wretched and peevish fellow is this [[King]] of England, to mope with his fat-brained followers so far out of his knowledge. |
| CONSTABLE | If the English had any apprehension, they would run away.<br>ORLÉANS That they lack—for if their heads had any intellectual armour, they could never wear such heavy headpieces. |
| RAMBURES | That island of England breeds very valiant creatures. Their mastiffs are of unmatchable [[Courage\|courage]].<br>ORLÉANS Foolish curs, that run winking into the mouth of a Russian bear, and have their heads crushed like rotten apples. You may as [[Well\|well]] say, ‘That’s a valiant flea that dare eat his breakfast on the lip of a [[Lion\|lion]].’ |
| CONSTABLE | Just, just. And the men do sympathize with the mastiffs in robustious and rough coming on, leaving their wits with their wives. And then, give them great meals of beef, and iron and steel, they will eat like wolves and fight like devils.<br>ORLÉANS Ay, but these English are shrewdly out of beef. |
| CONSTABLE | Then shall we find tomorrow they have only stomachs to eat, and none to fight. Now is it [[Time\|time]] to arm. Come, shall we about it?<br>ORLÉANS<br>It is now two o’[[Clock\|clock]]. But let me see—by ten<br>We shall have each a hundred Englishmen. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 0

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Chorus]] |
| [[Chorus\|CHORUS]] | Now entertain conjecture of a [[Time\|time]]<br>When creeping murmur and the poring dark<br>Fills the wide vessel of the universe.<br>From camp to camp through the foul womb of [[Night\|night]]<br>The hum of either army stilly sounds,<br>That the fixed sentinels almost receive<br>The secret whispers of each other’s watch.<br>[[Fire]] answers [[Fire\|fire]], and through their paly flames<br>Each battle sees the other’s umbered face.<br>Steed threatens steed, in high and boastful neighs<br>Piercing the [[Night\|night]]’s dull ear, and from the tents<br>The armourers, accomplishing the knights,<br>With busy hammers closing rivets up,<br>Give dreadful note of preparation.<br>The country cocks do crow, the clocks do toll<br>And the third hour of drowsy morning name.<br>Proud of their numbers and secure in [[Soul\|soul]],<br>The confident and overlusty French<br>Do the low-rated English play at dice,<br>And chide the cripple tardy-gaited [[Night\|night]],<br>Who like a foul and ugly [[Witch\|witch]] doth limp<br>So tediously away. The poor condemned English,<br>Like sacrifices, by their watchful fires<br>Sit patiently and inly ruminate<br>The morning’s danger; and their gesture sad,<br>Investing lank lean cheeks and war-worn coats,<br>Presented them unto the gazing [[Moon\|moon]]<br>So many horrid [[Ghosts\|ghosts]]. O now, who will behold<br>The royal captain of this ruined band<br>Walking from watch to watch, from tent to tent,<br>Let him cry, ‘Praise and glory on his head!’<br>For forth he goes and visits all his host,<br>Bids them good morrow with a modest smile<br>And calls them brothers, friends, and countrymen.<br>Upon his royal face there is no note<br>How dread an army hath enrounded him;<br>Nor doth he dedicate one jot of colour<br>Unto the weary and all-watchèd [[Night\|night]],<br>But freshly looks and overbears attaint<br>With cheerful semblance and sweet majesty,<br>That every wretch, pining and pale before,<br>Beholding him, plucks comfort from his looks.<br>A largess universal, like the sun,<br>His liberal eye doth give to everyone,<br>Thawing cold [[Fear\|fear]], that mean and gentle all<br>Behold, as may unworthiness define,<br>A little touch of Harry in the [[Night\|night]].<br>And so our scene must to the battle fly,<br>Where O for pity, we shall much disgrace,<br>With four or five most vile and ragged foils,<br>Right ill-disposed in brawl ridiculous,<br>The name of Agincourt. Yet sit and see,<br>Minding true things by what their mock’ries be. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Harry and the [[Duke of Gloucester]], then the Duke of [[Clarence]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | [[Gloucester]], ’tis true that we are in great danger;<br>The greater therefore should our [[Courage\|courage]] be.<br>Good morrow, brother [[Clarence]]. God Almighty!<br>There is some [[Soul\|soul]] of goodness in things evil,<br>Would men observingly distil it out—<br>For our bad neighbour makes us early stirrers,<br>Which is both healthful and good husbandry.<br>Besides, they are our outward consciences,<br>And preachers to us all, admonishing<br>That we should dress us fairly for our end.<br>Thus may we gather honey from the weed<br>And make a moral of the devil himself. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Thomas Erpingham |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Good morrow, old Sir Thomas Erpingham.<br>A good soft pillow for that good white head<br>Were better than a churlish turf of France. |
| ERPINGHAM | Not so, my liege. This lodging likes me better,<br>Since I may say, ‘Now lie I like a [[King\|king]].’ |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | ’Tis good for men to [[Love\|love]] their present pains<br>Upon example. So the spirit is eased,<br>And when the mind is quickened, out of [[Doubt\|doubt]]<br>The organs, though defunct and dead before,<br>Break up their drowsy [[Grave\|grave]] and newly move<br>With casted slough and fresh legerity.<br>Lend me thy cloak, Sir Thomas.<br>He puts on Erpingham’s cloak<br>Brothers both,<br>Commend me to the princes in our camp.<br>Do my good morrow to them, and anon<br>[[Desire]] them all to my pavilion. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | We shall, my liege. |
| ERPINGHAM | Shall I attend your grace? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | No, my good knight. Go with my brothers to my lords of England. I and my bosom must [[debate]] awhile, And then I would no other company. |
| ERPINGHAM | The Lord in heaven bless thee, noble Harry. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | God-a-[[Mercy\|mercy]], old heart, thou speak’st cheerfully. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[King]] Harry |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Pistol]] to him |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Qui vous là? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | A friend. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Discuss unto me: art thou officer,<br>Or art thou base, common, and popular? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I am a gentleman of a company. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Trail’st thou the puissant pike? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Even so. What are you? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | As good a gentleman as the Emperor. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Then you are a better than the [[King]]. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | The [[King]]’s a bawcock and a heart-of-gold,<br>A lad of life, an imp of fame,<br>Of parents good, of fist most valiant.<br>I kiss his dirty shoe, and from heartstring<br>I [[Love\|love]] the lovely bully. What is thy name? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Harry le roi. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Leroi? A Cornish name. Art thou of Cornish crew? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | No, I am a Welshman. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Know’st thou [[Fluellen]]? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Yes. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Tell him I’ll knock his leek about his pate<br>Upon Saint Davy’s day. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Do not you wear your dagger in your cap that day, lest he knock that about yours. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Art thou his friend? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | And his kinsman too. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | The fico for thee then. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I thank you. God be with you. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | My name is [[Pistol]] called. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | It sorts [[Well\|well]] with your fierceness. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Pistol]] Enter Captains [[Fluellen]] and [[Gower]] severally. [[King]] Harry stands apart |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Captain [[Fluellen]]! |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | So! In the name of Jesu [[Christ]], speak fewer. It is the greatest admiration in the universal world, when the true and ancient prerogatifs and laws of the wars is not kept. If you would take the pains but to examine the wars of [[Pompey]] the Great, you shall find, I warrant you, that there is no tiddle-taddle nor pibble-babble in [[Pompey]]’s camp. I warrant you, you shall find the ceremonies of the wars, and the cares of it, and the forms of it, and the sobriety of it, and the modesty of it, to be otherwise. |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Why, the enemy is loud. You hear him all [[Night\|night]]. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | If the enemy is an ass and a [[Fool\|fool]] and a prating coxcomb, is it meet, think you, that we should also, look you, be an ass and a [[Fool\|fool]] and a prating coxcomb? In your own [[Conscience\|conscience]] now? |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | I will speak lower. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | I pray you and beseech you that you will. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Fluellen]] and [[Gower]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Though it appear a little out of fashion,<br>There is much care and valour in this Welshman. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter three soldiers: John Bates, Alexander [[Court]], and [[Michael]] Williams |
| [[Court\|COURT]] | Brother John Bates, is not that the morning which breaks yonder? |
| BATES | I think it be. But we have no great cause to [[Desire\|desire]] the approach of day. |
| WILLIAM | We see yonder the beginning of the day, but I think we shall never see the end of it.—Who goes there? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | A friend. |
| WILLIAM | Under what captain serve you? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Under Sir Thomas Erpingham. |
| WILLIAM | A good old commander and a most kind gentleman. I pray you, what thinks he of our estate? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Even as men wrecked upon a sand, that look to be washed off the next tide. |
| BATES | He hath not told his thought to the [[King]]? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | No, nor it is not meet he should. For though I speak it to you, I think the [[King]] is but a man, as I am. The violet smells to him as it doth to me; the element shows to him as it doth to me. All his senses have but human conditions. His ceremonies laid by, in his [[Nakedness\|nakedness]] he appears but a man, and though his affections are higher mounted than ours, yet when they stoop, they stoop with the like wing. Therefore, when he sees reason of fears, as we do, his fears, out of [[Doubt\|doubt]], be of the same relish as ours are. Yet, in reason, no man should possess him with any appearance of [[Fear\|fear]], lest he, by showing it, should dishearten his army. |
| BATES | He may show what outward [[Courage\|courage]] he will, but I believe, as cold a [[Night\|night]] as ’tis, he could wish himself in Thames up to the neck. And so I would he were, and I by him, at all adventures, so we were quit here. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | By my troth, I will speak my [[Conscience\|conscience]] of the [[King]]. I think he would not wish himself anywhere but where he is. |
| BATES | Then I would he were here [[Alone\|alone]]. So should he be sure to be ransomed, and a many poor men’s lives saved. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I dare say you [[Love\|love]] him not so ill to wish him here [[Alone\|alone]], howsoever you speak this to feel other men’s minds. Methinks I could not die anywhere so contented as in the [[King]]’s company, his cause being just and his quarrel honourable. |
| WILLIAMS | That’s more than we know. |
| BATES | Ay, or more than we should seek after. For we know enough if we know we are the [[King]]’s subjects. If his cause be wrong, our obedience to the [[King]] wipes the crime of it out of us. |
| WILLIAMS | But if the cause be not good, the [[King]] himself hath a heavy reckoning to make, when all those legs and arms and heads chopped off in a battle shall join together at the latter day, and cry all, ‘We died at such a ptace’—some swearing, some crying for a surgeon, some upon their wives left poor behind them, some upon the debts they owe, some upon their children rawly left. I am afeard there are few die [[Well\|well]] that die in a battle, for how can they charitably dispose of anything, when [[Blood\|blood]] is their argument? Now, if these men do not die [[Well\|well]], it will be a black matter for the [[King]] that led them to it—who to disobey were against all proportion of subjection. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | So, if a son that is by his father sent about merchandise do sinfully miscarry upon the sea, the imputation of his wickedness, by your rule, should be imposed upon his father, that sent him. Or if a servant, under his master’s command transporting a sum of money, be assailed by robbers, and die in many irreconciled iniquities, you may call the business of the master the author of the servant’s damnation. But this is not so. The [[King]] is not bound to answer the particular endings of his soldiers, the father of his son, nor the master of his servant, for they purpose not their deaths when they propose their services. Besides, there is no [[King\|king]], be his cause never so spotless, if it come to the arbitrament of swords, can try it out with all unspotted soldiers. Some, peradventure, have on them the [[Guilt\|guilt]] of premeditated and contrived murder; some, of beguiling virgins with the broken seals of perjury; some, making the wars their bulwark, that have before gored the gentle bosom of peace with pillage and robbery. Now, if these men have defeated the law and outrun native punishment, though they can outstrip men, they have no wings to fly from God. War is his beadle. War is his vengeance. So that here men are punished for before-breach of the [[King]]’s laws, in now the [[King]]’s quarrel. Where they feared the [[Death\|death]], they have borne life away; and where they would be safe, they perish. Then if they die unprovided, no more is the [[King]] guilty of their damnation than he was before guilty of those impieties for the which they are now visited. Every subject’s duty is the [[King]]’s, but every subject’s [[Soul\|soul]] is his own. Therefore should every soldier in the wars do as every sick man in his bed: wash every mote out of his [[Conscience\|conscience]]. And dying so, [[Death\|death]] is to him advantage; or not dying, the [[Time\|time]] was blessedly lost wherein such preparation was gained. And in him that escapes, it were not sin to think that, making God so free an offer, he let him outlive that day to see his [[Greatness\|greatness]] and to teach others how they should prepare. |
| *(didascalia)* | BATES ’Tis certain, every man that dies ill, the ill upon his own head. The [[King]] is not to answer it. I do not [[Desire\|desire]] he should answer for me, and yet I determine to fight lustily for him. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I myself heard the [[King]] say he would not be ransomed. |
| WILLIAMS | Ay, he said so, to make us fight cheerfully, but when our throats are cut he may be ransomed, and we ne’er the wiser. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | If I live to see it, I will never trust his word after. |
| WILLIAMS | You pay him then! That’s a perilous shot out of an elder-gun, that a poor and a private displeasure can do against a monarch. You may as [[Well\|well]] go about to turn the sun to ice with fanning in his face with a peacock’s feather. You’ll never trust his word after! Come, ’tis a foolish saying. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Your reproof is something too round. I should be angry with you, if the [[Time\|time]] were convenient. |
| WILLIAMS | Let it be a quarrel between us, if you live. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I embrace it. |
| WILLIAM | How shall I know thee again? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Give me any gage of thine, and I will wear it in my bonnet. Then if ever thou darest acknowledge it, I will make it my quarrel. |
| WILLIAMS | Here’s my glove. Give me another of thine. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | There. They exchange gloves |
| WILLIAM | This will I also wear in my cap. If ever thou come to me and say, after tomorrow, ‘This is my glove’, by this hand I will take thee a box on the ear. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | If ever I live to see it, I will challenge it. |
| WILLIAM | Thou darest as [[Well\|well]] be hanged. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | [[Well]], I will do it, though I take thee in the [[King]]’s company. |
| WILLIAMS | Keep thy word. Fare thee [[Well\|well]]. |
| BATES | Be friends, you English fools, be friends. We have French quarrels enough, if you could tell how to reckon. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Indeed, the French may lay twenty French crowns to one they will beat us, for they bear them on their shoulders. But it is no English treason to cut French crowns, and tomorrow the [[King]] himself will be a clipper. Exeunt soldiers Upon the [[King]]. ‘Let us our lives, our souls, our debts, our care-full wives, Our children, and our sins, lay on the [[King]].’ We must bear all. O hard condition, Twin-born with [[Greatness\|greatness]]: subject to the breath Of every [[Fool\|fool]], whose sense no more can feel But his own wringing. What infinite heartsease Must kings neglect that private men enjoy? And what have kings that privates have not too, Save ceremony, save general ceremony? And what art thou, thou idol ceremony? What kind of god art thou, that suffer‘st more Of mortal griefs than do thy worshippers? What are thy rents? What are thy comings-in? O ceremony, show me but thy worth. What is thy [[Soul\|soul]] of adoration? Art thou aught else but place, degree, and form, Creating awe and [[Fear\|fear]] in other men? Wherein thou art less happy, being feared, Than they in fearing. What drink’st thou oft, instead of homage sweet, But poisoned flattery? O be sick, great [[Greatness\|greatness]], And bid thy ceremony give thee cure. Think‘st thou the fiery fever will go out With titles blown from adulation? Will it give place to flexure and low bending? Canst thou, when thou command’st the beggar’s knee, Command the health of it? No, thou proud dream That play‘st so subtly with a [[King\|king]]’s repose; I am a [[King\|king]] that find thee, and I know ’Tis not the balm, the sceptre, and the ball, The [[Sword\|sword]], the mace, the [[Crown\|crown]] imperial, The intertissued robe of gold and pearl, The farced title running fore the [[King\|king]], The throne he sits on, nor the tide of pomp That beats upon the high shore of this world—No, not all these, thrice-gorgeous ceremony, Not all these, laid in bed majestical, Can [[Sleep\|sleep]] so soundly as the wretched slave Who with a [[Body\|body]] filled and vacant mind Gets him to rest, crammed with distressful bread; Never sees horrid [[Night\|night]], the [[Child\|child]] of hell, But like a lackey from the rise to set Sweats in the eye of Phoebus, and all [[Night\|night]] Sleeps in Elysium; next day, after [[Dawn\|dawn]] Doth rise and help [[Hyperion]] to his [[Horse\|horse]], And follows so the ever-running year With profitable labour to his [[Grave\|grave]]. And but for ceremony such a wretch, Winding up days with toil and nights with [[Sleep\|sleep]], Had the forehand and vantage of a [[King\|king]]. The slave, a member of the country’s peace, Enjoys it, but in gross brain little wots What watch the [[King]] keeps to maintain the peace, Whose hours the peasant best advantages. Enter Sir Thomas Erpingham |
| ERPINGHAM | My lord, your nobles, jealous of your absence,<br>Seek through your camp to find you. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Good old knight,<br>Collect them all together at my tent.<br>I’ll be before thee. |
| ERPINGHAM | I shall do’t, my lord. Exit |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | O God of battles, steel my soldiers’ hearts.<br>Possess them not with [[Fear\|fear]]. Take from them now<br>The sense of reck‘ning, ere th’opposèd numbers<br>Pluck their hearts from them. Not today, O Lord,<br>O not today, think not upon the fault<br>My father made in compassing the [[Crown\|crown]].<br>I [[Richard]]’s [[Body\|body]] have interred new,<br>And on it have bestowed more contrite [[Tears\|tears]]<br>Than from it issued forced drops of [[Blood\|blood]].<br>Five hundred poor have I in yearly pay<br>Who twice a day their withered hands hold up<br>Toward heaven to pardon [[Blood\|blood]]. And I have built<br>Two chantries, where the sad and solemn priests<br>Sing still for [[Richard]]’s [[Soul\|soul]]. More will I do,<br>Though all that I can do is nothing worth,<br>Since that my penitence comes after ill,<br>Imploring pardon. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of Gloucester]] |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | My liege. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | My brother [[Gloucester]]’s voice? Ay.<br>I know thy errand, I will go with thee.<br>The day, my friends, and all things stay for me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Dukes of Bourbonand Orléans, and Lord |
| *(didascalia)* | Rambures<br>ORLÉANS The sun doth gild our armour. Up, my lords! |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBONMonte cheval! My [[Horse\|horse]]! Varlet, lacquais! Ha! |
| *(didascalia)* | ORLÉANS O brave spirit! |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Via les eaux et terre! |
| *(didascalia)* | ORLÉANS Rien plus? L’air et feu! |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Cieux, cousin Orléans! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Constable |
| *(didascalia)* | Now, my Lord Constable! |
| CONSTABLE | Hark how our steeds for present service neigh. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON |
| CONSTABLE | Mount them and make incision in their hides,<br>That their hot [[Blood\|blood]] may spin in English eyes<br>And dout them with superfluous [[Courage\|courage]]. Ha! |
| RAMBURES | What, will you have them weep our horses’ [[Blood\|blood]]?<br>How shall we then behold their natural [[Tears\|tears]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | The English are embattled, you French peers. |
| CONSTABLE | To [[Horse\|horse]], you gallant princes, straight to [[Horse\|horse]]!<br>Do but behold yon poor and starved band,<br>And your fair show shall suck away their souls,<br>Leaving them but the shells and husks of men.<br>There is not work enough for all our hands,<br>Scarce [[Blood\|blood]] enough in all their sickly veins<br>To give each naked curtal-axe a stain<br>That our French gallants shall today draw out<br>And sheathe for lack of sport. Let us but blow on<br>them,<br>The vapour of our valour will o‘erturn them.<br>’Tis positive ’gainst all exceptions, lords,<br>That our superfluous lackeys and our peasants,<br>Who in unnecessary action swarm<br>About our squares of battle, were enough<br>To purge this field of such a hilding foe,<br>Though we upon this mountain’s basis by<br>Took stand for idle speculation,<br>But that our honours must not. What’s to say?<br>A very little little let us do<br>And all is done. Then let the trumpets sound<br>The tucket sonance and the note to mount,<br>For our approach shall so much dare the field<br>That England shall couch down in [[Fear\|fear]] and yield. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lord Grandpré |
| CONSTABLE | GRANDPRÉ<br>Why do you stay so long, my lords of France?<br>Yon island carrions, desperate of their bones,<br>Ill-favouredly become the morning field.<br>Their ragged curtains poorly are let loose<br>And our air shakes them passing scornfully.<br>Big Mars seems bankrupt in their beggared host<br>And faintly through a rusty beaver peeps.<br>The horsemen sit like fixed candlesticks<br>With torchstaves in their hands, and their poor jades<br>Lob down their heads, drooping the hides and hips,<br>The gum down-roping from their pale dead eyes,<br>And in their palled dull mouths the gimmaled bit<br>Lies foul with chewed grass, still and motionless.<br>And their executors, the knavish crows,<br>Fly o’er them all impatient for their hour.<br>Description cannot suit itself in words<br>To demonstrate the life of such a battle<br>In life so lifeless as it shows itself. |
| CONSTABLE | They have said their prayers, and they stay for [[Death\|death]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON |
| CONSTABLE | Shall we go send them dinners and fresh suits<br>And give their fasting horses provender,<br>And after fight with them? |
| CONSTABLE | I stay but for my guidon. To the field!<br>I will the banner from a trumpet take<br>And use it for my haste. Come, come away!<br>The sun is high, and we outwear the day. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Dukes of [[Gloucester]], [[Clarence]], and Exeter, the Earls of Salisbury and Warwick, and Sir Thomas Erpingham, with all the host |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Where is the [[King]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | The [[King]] himself is rode to view their battle.<br>[WARWICK]<br>Of fighting men they have full threescore thousand. |
| EXETER | There’s five to one. Besides, they all are fresh. |
| SALISBURY | God’s arm strike with us! ‘Tis a fearful odds.<br>God b’wi’ you, princes all. I’ll to my charge.<br>If we no more meet till we meet in heaven,<br>Then joyfully, my noble Lord of [[Clarence]],<br>My dear Lord [[Gloucester]], and my good Lord Exeter,<br>And (to Warwick) my kind kinsman, warriors all,<br>adieu. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] |
| SALISBURY | Farewell, good Salisbury, and good luck go with thee. |
| EXETER | Farewell, kind lord. Fight valiantly today—<br>And yet I do thee wrong to mind thee of it,<br>For thou art framed of the firm [[Truth\|truth]] of valour. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Salisbury |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] |
| EXETER | He is as full of valour as of kindness,<br>Princely in both. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Harry, behind |
| *(didascalia)* | WARWICK |
| EXETER | O that we now had here<br>But one ten thousand of those men in England<br>That do no work today. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | What’s he that wishes so?<br>My cousin Warwick? No, my fair cousin.<br>If we are marked to die, we are enough<br>To do our country loss; and if to live,<br>The fewer men, the greater share of [[Honour\|honour]].<br>God’s will, I pray thee wish not one man more.<br>By Jove, I am not covetous for gold,<br>Nor care I who doth feed upon my cost;<br>It ernes me not if men my garments wear;<br>Such outward things dwell not in my desires.<br>But if it be a sin to covet [[Honour\|honour]]<br>I am the most offending [[Soul\|soul]] alive.<br>No, [[Faith\|faith]], my coz, wish not a man from England.<br>God’s peace, I would not lose so great an [[Honour\|honour]]<br>As one man more methinks would share from me<br>For the best [[Hope\|hope]] I have. O do not wish one more.<br>Rather proclaim it presently through my host<br>That he which hath no stomach to this fight,<br>Let him depart. His passport shall be made<br>And crowns for convoy put into his purse.<br>We would not die in that man’s company<br>That fears his fellowship to die with us.<br>This day is called the Feast of Crispian.<br>He that outlives this day and comes safe [[Home\|home]]<br>Will stand a-tiptoe when this day is named<br>And rouse him at the name of Crispian.<br>He that shall see this day and live t‘old age<br>Will yearly on the vigil feast his neighbours<br>And say, ’Tomorrow is Saint Crispian.’<br>Then will he strip his sleeve and show his scars<br>And say, ’These wounds I had on Crispin’s day.’<br>Old men forget; yet all shall be forgot,<br>But he’ll remember, with advantages,<br>What feats he did that day. Then shall our names,<br>Familiar in his mouth as household words—<br>Harry the [[King]], Bedford and Exeter,<br>Warwick and Talbot, Salisbury and [[Gloucester]]—<br>Be in their flowing cups freshly remembered.<br>This story shall the good man teach his son,<br>And Crispin Crispian shall ne’er go by<br>From this day to the ending of the world<br>But we in it shall be remembered,<br>We few, we happy few, we band of brothers.<br>For he today that sheds his [[Blood\|blood]] with me<br>Shall be my brother; be he ne’er so vile,<br>This day shall gentle his condition.<br>And gentlemen in England now abed<br>Shall think themselves accursed they were not here,<br>And hold their manhoods cheap whiles any speaks<br>That fought with us upon Saint Crispin’s day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Salisbury]] |
| SALISBURY | My sovereign lord, bestow yourself with [[Speed\|speed]].<br>The French are bravely in their battles set<br>And will with all expedience charge on us. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | All things are ready if our minds be so. |
| *(didascalia)* | WARWICK Perish the man whose mind is backward now. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Thou dost not wish more help from England, coz? |
| *(didascalia)* | WARWICK |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | God’s will, my liege, would you and I [[Alone\|alone]],<br>Without more help, could fight this royal battle. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Why now thou hast unwished five thousand men,<br>Which likes me better than to wish us one.—<br>You know your places. God be with you all. |
| *(didascalia)* | Tucket. Enter Montjoy |
| MONTJOY | Once more I come to know of thee, [[King]] Harry,<br>If for thy ransom thou wilt now compound<br>Before thy most assured overthrow.<br>For certainly thou art so near the gulf<br>Thou needs must be englutted. Besides, in [[Mercy\|mercy]]<br>The Constable desires thee thou wilt mind<br>Thy followers of repentance, that their souls<br>May make a peaceful and a sweet retire<br>From off these fields where, wretches, their poor<br>bodies<br>Must lie and fester. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Who hath sent thee now? |
| MONTJOY | The Constable of France. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I pray thee bear my former answer back.<br>Bid them achieve me, and then sell my bones.<br>Good God, why should they mock poor fellows thus?<br>The man that once did sell the [[Lion\|lion]]’s skin<br>While the beast lived, was killed with hunting him. 95<br>A many of our bodies shall no [[Doubt\|doubt]]<br>Find native graves, upon the which, I trust,<br>Shall witness live in brass of this day’s work.<br>And those that leave their valiant bones in France,<br>Dying like men, though buried in your dunghills<br>They shall be famed. For there the sun shall greet<br>them<br>And draw their honours reeking up to heaven,<br>Leaving their earthly parts to choke your clime,<br>The smell whereof shall breed a plague in France.<br>Mark then abounding valour in our English,<br>That, being dead, like to the bullets grazing<br>Break out into a second course of mischief,<br>Killing in relapse of [[Mortality\|mortality]].<br>Let me speak proudly. Tell the Constable<br>We are but warriors for the working day.<br>Our gayness and our gilt are all besmirched<br>With rainy marching in the painful field.<br>There’s not a piece of feather in our host—<br>Good argument, I [[Hope\|hope]], we will not fly—<br>And [[Time\|time]] hath worn us into slovenry.<br>But by the mass, our hearts are in the trim.<br>And my poor soldiers tell me, yet ere [[Night\|night]]<br>They’ll be in fresher robes, as they will pluck<br>The gay new coats o‘er your French soldiers’ heads,<br>And turn them out of service. If they do this—<br>As if God please, they shall—my ransom then<br>Will soon be levied. Herald, save thou thy labour.<br>Come thou no more for ransom, gentle herald.<br>They shall have none, I swear, but these my joints—<br>Which if they have as I will leave ’em them,<br>Shall yield them little. Tell the Constable. |
| MONTJOY | I shall, [[King]] Harry. And so fare thee [[Well\|well]].<br>Thou never shalt hear herald any more. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I [[Fear\|fear]] thou wilt once more come for a ransom. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Montjoy |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of York]] |
| YORK | My lord, most humbly on my knee I beg<br>The leading of the vanguard. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Take it, brave York.—Now soldiers, march away,<br>And how thou pleasest, God, dispose the day. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Excursions. Enter [[Pistol]], a French soldier, and the Boy |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Yield, cur. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | je pense que vous êtes le gentilhomme de bon qualité. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Qualité? ‘Calin o custure me!’<br><br>Art thou a gentleman? What is thy name? Discuss. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | O Seigneur Dieu! |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] aside |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | O Seigneur Dew should be a gentleman.—<br>Perpend my words, O Seigneur Dew, and mark:<br>O Seigneur Dew, thou diest, on point of fox,<br>Except, O Seigneur, thou do give to me<br>Egregious ransom. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | O prenez miséricorde! Ayez pitie de moi! |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | ‘Moy’ shall not serve, I will have forty ‘moys’,<br>Or I will fetch thy rim out at thy throat<br>In drops of crimson [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | Est-il impossible d’échapper la force de ton bras? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Brass, cur? Thou damned and luxurious mountain<br>goat,<br>Offer’st me brass? |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | O pardonne-moi! |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Sayst thou me so? Is that a ton of moys?—<br>Come hither boy. Ask me this slave in French<br>What is his name. |
| BOY | Écoutez: comment êtes-vous appelé? |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | Monsieur le Fer. |
| BOY | He says his name is Master Fer. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Master Fer? I’ll fer him, and firk him, and ferret him.<br>Discuss the same in French unto him. |
| BOY | I do not know the French for fer and ferret and firk. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Bid him prepare, for I will cut his throat. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | Que dit-il, monsieur? |
| BOY | Il me commande à vous dire que vous faites vous prêt, car ce soldat ici est dispose tout à cette heure de couper votre gorge. 35 |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Oui, couper la gorge, par ma foi,<br><br>Peasant, unless thou give me crowns, brave crowns;<br>Or mangled shalt thou be by this my [[Sword\|sword]]. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | O je vous supplie, pour l’amour de Dieu, me pardonner. Je suis le gentilhomme de bonne maison. Gardez ma vie, et je vous donnerai deux cents écus. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | What are his words? |
| BOY | He prays you to save his life. He is a gentleman of a good [[house]], and for his ransom he will give you two hundred crowns. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Tell him, my fury shall abate, and I the crowns will take. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | Petit monsieur, que dit-il? |
| BOY | Encore qu’il est centre son jurement de pardonner aucun prisonnier; neanmoins, pour les ecus que vous lui ci promettez, il est content à vous donner la liberté, le franchisement. |
| FRENCH SOLDIER | (kneeling to [[Pistol]]) Sur mes genoux je vous donne mille remerciements, et je m‘estime heureux que j’ai tombe entre les mains d‘un chevalier, comme je pense, le plus brave, vaillant, et treis-distingué seigneur d’Angleterre. [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] Expound unto me, boy. |
| BOY | He gives you upon his knees a thousand thanks, and he esteems himself happy that he hath fallen into the hands of one, as he thinks, the most brave, valorous, and thrice-worthy seigneur of England. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | As I suck [[Blood\|blood]], I will some [[Mercy\|mercy]] show.<br>Follow me. |
| BOY | Suivez-vous le grand capitaine. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Pistol]] and French Soldier |
| BOY | I did never know so full a voice issue from so empty a<br>heart. But the saying is true: ‘The empty vessel makes<br>the greatest sound.’ Bardolph and Nim had ten times<br>more valour than this roaring devil i’th’ old play, that<br>everyone may pare his nails with a wooden dagger,<br>and they are both hanged, and so would this be, if he<br>durst steal anything adventurously. I must stay with<br>the lackeys with the luggage of our camp. The French<br>might have a good prey of us, if he knew of it, for there<br>is none to guard it but boys. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Constable, the Dukes of |
| *(didascalia)* | Orléans and Bourbon, and Lord Rambures |
| CONSTABLE | O diable!<br>ORLÉANS O Seigneur! Le jour est perdu, tout est perdu! |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON |
| CONSTABLE | Mort de ma vie! All is confounded, all.<br>Reproach and everlasting shame<br>Sits mocking in our plumes.<br>A short alarum<br>O mechante fortune!– (To Rambures) Do not run away. |
| *(didascalia)* | ORLÉANS |
| CONSTABLE | We are enough yet living in the field<br>To smother up the English in our throngs,<br>If any order might be thought upon. |
| BOURBON | The devil take order. Once more back again!<br>And he that will not follow Bourbon now,<br>Let him go [[Home\|home]], and with his cap in hand<br>Like a base leno hold the chamber door<br>Whilst by a slave no gentler than my dog<br>His fairest daughter is contaminated. |
| CONSTABLE | Disorder that hath spoiled us friend us now.<br>Let us on heaps go offer up our lives. |
| BOURBON | I’ll to the throng.<br>Let life be short, else shame will be too long. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter [[King]] Harry and his train, with prisoners |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | [[Well]] have we done, thrice-valiant countrymen.<br>But all’s not done; yet keep the French the field.<br>[Enter the [[Duke of Exeter]]] |
| EXETER | The [[Duke of York]] commends him to your majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Lives he, good uncle? Thrice within this hour<br>I saw him down, thrice up again and fighting.<br>From helmet to the spur, all [[Blood\|blood]] he was. |
| EXETER | In which array, brave soldier, doth he lie,<br>Larding the plain. And by his bloody side,<br>Yokefellow to his [[Honour\|honour]]-owing wounds,<br>The noble [[Earl of Suffolk]] also lies.<br>Suffolk first died, and York, all haggled over,<br>Comes to him, where in gore he lay insteeped,<br>And takes him by the beard, kisses the gashes<br>That bloodily did yawn upon his face,<br>And cries aloud, ‘Tarry, dear cousin Suffolk.<br>My [[Soul\|soul]] shall thine keep company to heaven.<br>Tarry, sweet [[Soul\|soul]], for mine, then fly abreast,<br>As in this glorious and [[Well\|well]]-foughten field<br>We kept together in our chivalry.’<br>Upon these words I came and cheered him up.<br>He smiled me in the face, raught me his hand,<br>And with a feeble grip says, ‘Dear my lord,<br>Commend my service to my sovereign.’<br>So did he turn, and over Suffolk’s neck<br>He threw his wounded arm, and kissed his lips,<br>And so espoused to [[Death\|death]], with [[Blood\|blood]] he sealed<br>A testament of noble-ending [[Love\|love]].<br>The pretty and sweet manner of it forced<br>Those waters from me which I would have stopped.<br>But I had not so much of man in me,<br>And all my mother came into mine eyes<br>And gave me up to [[Tears\|tears]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I blame you not,<br>For hearing this I must perforce compound<br>With mistful eyes, or they will issue too. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | But hark, what new alarum is this same?<br>The French have reinforced their scattered men.<br>Then every soldier kill his prisoners.<br>[The soldiers kill their prisoners]<br>Give the word through. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] Coup’ la gorge. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Captains [[Fluellen]] and [[Gower]] |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Kill the poys and the luggage! ‘Tis expressly against the law of arms. ’Tis as arrant a piece of knavery, mark you now, as can be offert. In your [[Conscience\|conscience]] now, is it not? |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | ‘Tis certain there’s not a boy left alive. And the cowardly rascals that ran from the battle ha’ done this slaughter. Besides, they have burned and carried away all that was in the [[King]]’s tent; wherefore the [[King]] most worthily hath caused every soldier to cut his prisoner’s throat. O ’tis a gallant [[King\|king]]. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Ay, he was porn at Monmouth. Captain [[Gower]], what call you the town’s name where Alexander the Pig was born? |
| GLOWER | Alexander the Great. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Why I pray you, is not ‘pig’ great? The pig or the great or the mighty or the huge or the magnanimous are all one reckonings, save the phrase is a little variations. |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | I think Alexander the Great was born in Macedon. His father was called Philip of Macedon, as I take it. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | I think it is e‘en Macedon where Alexander is porn. I tell you, captain, if you look in the maps of the world I warrant you sail find, in the comparisons between Macedon and Monmouth, that the situations, look you, is both alike. There is a [[River\|river]] in Macedon, and there is also moreover a [[River\|river]] at Monmouth. It is called Wye at Monmouth, but it is out of my prains what is the name of the other [[River\|river]]—but ’tis all one, ’tis alike as my fingers is to my fingers, and there is salmons in both. If you mark Alexander’s life [[Well\|well]], Harry of Monmouth’s life is come after it indifferent [[Well\|well]]. For there is figures in all things. Alexander, God knows, and you know, in his rages and his furies and his wraths and his cholers and his moods and his displeasures and his indignations, and also being a little intoxicates in his prains, did in his ales and his angers, look you, kill his best friend Cleitus— |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Our [[King]] is not like him in that. He never killed any of his friends. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | It is not [[Well\|well]] done, mark you now, to take the tales out of my mouth ere it is made an end and finished. I speak but in the figures and comparisons of it. As Alexander killed his friend Cleitus, being in his ales and his cups, so also Harry Monmouth, being in his right wits and his good judgements, turned away the fat knight with the great-belly doublet—he was full of jests and gipes and knaveries and mocks—I have forgot his name. |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Sir John [[Falstaff]]. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | That is he. I’ll tell you, there is good men porn at Monmouth. |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Here comes his majesty. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter [[King]] Harry and the English army, with the Duke of Bourbon, the Duke of Orléans, and other prisoners. Flourish |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I was not angry since I came to France<br>Until this instant. Take a trumpet, herald;<br>Ride thou unto the horsemen on yon [[Hill\|hill]].<br>If they will fight with us, bid them come down,<br>Or void the field: they do offend our sight.<br>If they’ll do neither, we will come to them,<br>And make them skirr away as swift as stones<br>Enforced from the old Assyrian slings.<br>Besides, we’ll cut the throats of those we have,<br>And not a man of them that we shall take<br>Shall taste our [[Mercy\|mercy]]. Go and tell them so. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Montjoy |
| EXETER | Here comes the herald of the French, my liege. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | His eyes are humbler than they used to be. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | How now, what means this, herald? Know‘st thou<br>not<br>That I have fined these bones of mine for ransom?<br>Com’st thou again for ransom? |
| MONTJOY | No, great [[King]].<br>I come to thee for charitable licence,<br>That we may wander o’er this bloody field<br>To book our dead and then to bury them,<br>To sort our nobles from our common men—<br>For many of our princes, woe the while,<br>Lie drowned and soaked in mercenary [[Blood\|blood]].<br>So do our vulgar drench their peasant limbs<br>In [[Blood\|blood]] of princes, and our wounded steeds<br>Fret fetlock-deep in gore, and with wild rage<br>Jerk out their armed heels at their dead masters,<br>Killing them twice. O give us leave, great [[King]],<br>To view the field in safety, and dispose<br>Of their dead bodies. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I tell thee truly, herald,<br>I know not if the day be ours or no,<br>For yet a many of your horsemen peer<br>And gallop o’er the field. |
| MONTJOY | The day is yours. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Praised be God, and not our strength, for it.<br>What is this [[Castle\|castle]] called that stands hard by? |
| MONTJOY | They call it Agincourt. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Then call we this the field of Agincourt,<br>Fought on the day of Crispin Crispian. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Your grandfather of famous [[Memory\|memory]], an’t please your majesty, and your great-uncle Edward the Plack Prince of Wales, as I have read in the chronicles, fought a most prave pattle here in France. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | They did, [[Fluellen]]. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Your majesty says very true. If your majesties is remembered of it, the Welshmen did good service in a [[Garden\|garden]] where leeks did grow, wearing leeks in their Monmouth caps, which your majesty know to this hour is an honourable badge of the service. And I do believe your majesty takes no scorn to wear the leek upon Saint Tavy’s day. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I wear it for a memorable [[Honour\|honour]],<br>For I am Welsh, you know, good countryman. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | All the [[Water\|water]] in Wye cannot wash your majesty’s Welsh plood out of your pody, I can tell you that. God pless it and preserve it, as long as it pleases his grace, and his majesty too. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Thanks, good my countryman. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | By Jeshu, I am your majesty’s countryman. I care not who know it, I will confess it to all the world. I need not to be ashamed of your majesty, praised be God, so long as your majesty is an honest man. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | God keep me so. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Williams with a glove in his cap |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Our heralds go with him.<br>Bring me just notice of the numbers dead On both our parts. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Montjoy, [[Gower]],and an English herald |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Call yonder fellow hither. |
| EXETER | (to Williams) Soldier, you must come to the [[King]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Soldier, why wearest thou that glove in thy cap? |
| WILLIAM | An’t please your majesty, ’tis the gage of one that I should fight withal, if he be alive. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | An Englishman? |
| WILLIAMS | An’t please your majesty, a rascal, that swaggered with me last [[Night\|night]]—who, if a live, and ever dare to challenge this glove, I have sworn to take him a box o’th’ ear; or if I can see my glove in his cap—which he swore, as he was a soldier, he would wear if a lived—I will strike it out soundly. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | What think you, Captain [[Fluellen]]? Is it fit this soldier keep his oath? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | He is a craven and a [[Villain\|villain]] else, an’t please your majesty, in my [[Conscience\|conscience]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | It may be his enemy is a gentleman of great sort, quite from the answer of his degree. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Though he be as good a gentleman as the devil is, as [[Lucifer]] and Beelzebub himself, it is necessary, look your grace, that he keep his vow and his oath. If he be perjured, see you now, his reputation is as arrant a [[Villain\|villain]] and a Jack-sauce as ever his black shoe trod upon God’s ground and his earth, in my [[Conscience\|conscience]], law. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Then keep thy vow, sirrah, when thou meetest the fellow. |
| WILLIAMS | So I will, my liege, as I live. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Who serv’st thou under? |
| WILLIAM | Under Captain [[Gower]], my liege. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | [[Gower]] is a good captain, and is good knowledge and literatured in the wars. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Call him hither to me, soldier. |
| WILLIAM | I will, my liege. Exit |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | (giving him Williams’s other glove) Here, [[Fluellen]], wear thou this favour for me and stick it in thy cap. When Alençon and myself were down together, I plucked this glove from his helm. If any man challenge this, he is a friend to Alençon and an enemy to our person. If thou encounter any such, apprehend him, an thou dost me [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Your grace does me as great honours as can be desired in the hearts of his subjects. I would fain see the man that has but two legs that shall find himself aggriefed at this glove, that is all; but I would fain see it once. An’t please God of his grace, that I would see. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Know’st thou [[Gower]]? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | He is my dear friend, an’t please you. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Pray thee, go seek him and bring him to my tent. 165 |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | I will fetch him. Exit |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | My lord of Warwick and my brother [[Gloucester]],<br>Follow [[Fluellen]] closely at the heels.<br>The glove which I have given him for a favour<br>May haply purchase him a box o’th’ ear.<br>It is the soldier’s. I by bargain should<br>Wear it myself. Follow, good cousin Warwick.<br>If that the soldier strike him, as I judge<br>By his blunt bearing he will keep his word,<br>Some sudden mischief may arise of it,<br>For I do know [[Fluellen]] valiant<br>And touched with choler, hot as gunpowder,<br>And quickly will return an injury.<br>Follow, and see there be no harm between them.<br>Go you with me, uncle of Exeter. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 4, Scena 8

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Captain [[Gower]] and Williams |
| WILLIAMS | I warrant it is to knight you, captain. Enter Captain [[Fluellen]] |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | God’s will and his pleasure, captain, I beseech you now, come apace to the [[King]]. There is more good toward you, peradventure, than is in your knowledge to dream of. |
| WILLIAM | Sir, know you this glove? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Know the glove? I know the glove is a glove. |
| WILLIAM | Fplucking the glove from [[Fluellen]]’s cap] I know this, and thus I challenge it. He strikes [[Fluellen]] |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | God’s plood, and his! An arrant traitor as any’s in the universal world, or in France, or in England. |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | (to Williams) How now, sir? You [[Villain\|villain]]! |
| WILLIAM | Do you think I’ll be forsworn? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Stand away, Captain [[Gower]]. I will give treason his payment into plows, I warrant you.<br>Williams I am no traitor. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | That’s a lie in thy throat. I charge you in his majesty’s name, apprehend him. He’s a friend of the Duke Alençon’s. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Warwick]] and the [[Duke of Gloucester]] |
| WARWICK | How now, how now, what’s the matter? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | My lord of Warwick, here is—praised be God for it—a most contagious treason come to light, look you, as you shall [[Desire\|desire]] in a [[Summer\|summer]]’s day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Harry and the [[Duke of Exeter]] Here is his majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | How now, what is the matter? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | My liege, here is a [[Villain\|villain]] and a traitor that, look your grace, has struck the glove which your majesty is take out of the helmet of Alençon. |
| WILLIAMS | My liege, this was my gtove—here is the fellow of it—and he that I gave it to in change promised to wear it in his cap. I promised to strike him, if he did. I met this man with my glove in his cap, and I have been as good as my word. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Your majesty hear now, saving your majesty’s manhood, what an arrant rascally beggarly lousy knave it is. I [[Hope\|hope]] your majesty is pear me testimony and witness, and will avouchment that this is the glove of Alençon that your majesty is give me, in your [[Conscience\|conscience]] now. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Give me thy glove, soldier. Look, here is the fellow of it.<br>‘Twas I indeed thou promisèd’st to strike,<br>And thou hast given me most bitter terms. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | An’t please your majesty, let his neck answer for it, if there is any martial law in the world. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | How canst thou make me satisfaction? |
| WILLIAMS | All offences, my lord, come from the heart. Never came any from mine that might offend your majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | It was ourself thou didst abuse. |
| WILLIAMS | Your majesty came not like yourself. You appeared to me but as a [[Common Man\|common man]]. Witness the [[Night\|night]], your garments, your lowliness. And what your highness suffered under that shape, I beseech you take it for your own fault, and not mine, for had you been as I took you for, I made no offence. Therefore I beseech your highness pardon me. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Here, Uncle Exeter, fill this glove with crowns<br>And give it to this fellow.—Keep it, fellow,<br>And wear it for an [[Honour\|honour]] in thy cap<br>Till I do challenge it.—Give him the crowns.<br>—And captain, you must needs be friends with him. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | By this day and this light, the fellow has mettle enough in his betty.—Ho[d, there is twelve pence for you, and I pray you to serve God, and keep you out of prawls and prabbles and quarrels and dissensions, and I warrant you it is the better for you. |
| WILLIAMS | I will none of your money. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | It is with a good will. I can tell you, it will<br>serve you to mend your shoes. Come, wherefore should<br>you be so pashful? Your shoes is not so good. ’Tis a<br>good shilling, I warrant you, or I will change it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter an English Herald |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Now, herald, are the dead numbered? |
| HERALD | Here is the number of the slaughtered French. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | What prisoners of good sort are taken, uncle? |
| EXETER | Charles, Duke of Orléans, nephew to the [[King]];<br>Jean, Duke of Bourbon, and Lord Boucicault;<br>Of other lords and barons, knights and squires,<br>Full fifteen hundred, besides common men. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | This note doth tell me of ten thousand French<br>That in the field lie slain. Of princes in this number<br>And nobles bearing banners, there lie dead<br>One hundred twenty-six; added to these,<br>Of knights, esquires, and gallant gentlemen,<br>Eight thousand and four hundred, of the which<br>Five hundred were but yesterday dubbed knights.<br>So that in these ten thousand they have lost<br>There are but sixteen hundred mercenaries;<br>The rest are princes, barons, lords, knights, squires,<br>And gentlemen of [[Blood\|blood]] and quality.<br>The names of those their nobles that lie dead:<br>Charles Delabret, High Constable of France;<br>[[Jaques]] of Châtillon, Admiral of France;<br>The Master of the Crossbows, Lord Rambures;<br>Great-Master of France, the brave Sir Guiscard<br>[[Dauphin]];<br>Jean, Duke of Alençon; Antony, Duke of Brabant,<br>The brother to the Duke of Burgundy;<br>And Édouard, Duke of Bar; of lusty earls,<br>Grandpré and Roussi, Fauconbridge and Foix,<br>Beaumont and Marle, Vaudemont and Lestrelles.<br>Here was a royal fellowship of [[Death\|death]].<br>Where is the number of our English dead?<br>He is given another paper<br>Edward the [[Duke of York]], the [[Earl of Suffolk]],<br>Sir [[Richard]] Keighley, Davy Gam Esquire;<br>None else of name, and of all other men<br>But five-and-twenty. O God, thy arm was here,<br>And not to us, but to thy arm [[Alone\|alone]]<br>Ascribe we all. When, without stratagem,<br>But in plain shock and even play of battle,<br>Was ever known so great and little loss<br>On one part and on th’other? Take it God,<br>For it is none but thine. |
| EXETER | ’Tis wonderful. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Come, go we in procession to the village,<br>And be it [[Death\|death]] proclaimed through our host<br>To boast of this, or take that praise from God<br>Which is his only. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Is it not lawful, an’t please your majesty, to tell how many is killed? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Yes, captain, but with this acknowledgement,<br>That God fought for us. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Yes, in my [[Conscience\|conscience]], he did us great good. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Do we all holy rites:<br>Let there be sung Non nobis and Te Deum,<br><br>The dead with [[Charity\|charity]] enclosed in clay;<br>And then to Calais, and to England then,<br>Where ne’er from France arrived more-happy men. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 0

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Chorus]] |
| [[Chorus\|CHORUS]] | Vouchsafe to those that have not read the story<br>That I may prompt them—and of such as have,<br>I humbly pray them to admit th‘excuse<br>Of [[Time\|time]], of numbers, and due course of things,<br>Which cannot in their huge and proper life<br>Be here presented. Now we bear the [[King]]<br>Toward Calais. Grant him there; there seen,<br>Heave him away upon your winged thoughts<br>Athwart the sea. Behold, the English beach<br>Pales-in the flood, with men, maids, wives, and boys,<br>Whose shouts and claps out-voice the deep-mouthed<br>sea,<br>Which like a mighty whiffler fore the [[King]]<br>Seems to prepare his way. So let him land,<br>And solemnly see him set on to [[London]].<br>So swift a pace hath thought, that even now<br>You may imagine him upon Blackheath,<br>Where that his lords [[Desire\|desire]] him to have borne<br>His bruised helmet and his bended [[Sword\|sword]]<br>Before him through the [[City\|city]]; he forbids it,<br>Being free from vainness and [[Self\|self]]-glorious [[Pride\|pride]],<br>Giving full trophy, signal, and ostent<br>Quite from himself, to God. But now behold,<br>In the quick forge and working-[[house]] of thought,<br>How [[London]] doth pour out her citizens.<br>The Mayor and all his brethren, in best sort,<br>Like to the senators of th’antique Rome<br>With the plebeians swarming at their heels,<br>Go forth and fetch their conqu’ring Caesar in—<br>As, by a lower but high-loving likelihood,<br>Were now the General of our gracious Empress—<br>As in good [[Time\|time]] he may—from Ireland coming,<br>Bringing rebellion broached on his [[Sword\|sword]],<br>How many would the peaceful [[City\|city]] quit<br>To welcome him! Much more, and much more cause,<br>Did they this Harry. Now in [[London]] place him;<br>As yet the lamentation of the French<br>Invites the [[King]] of England’s stay at [[Home\|home]].<br>The Emperor’s coming in behalf of France, |
| *(didascalia)* | To order peace between them |
| *(didascalia)* |  and omit |
| [[Chorus\|CHORUS]] | All the occurrences, whatever chanced,<br>Till Harry’s back-return again to France.<br>There must we bring him, and myself have played<br>The interim by rememb‘ring you ’tis past.<br>Then brook abridgement, and your eyes advance,<br>After your thoughts, straight back again to France. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Captain [[Gower]] and Captain [[Fluellen]], with a leek in his cap and a cudgel |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Nay, that’s right. But why wear you your leek today? Saint Davy’s day is past. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | There is occasions and causes why and wherefore in all things. I will tell you, ass my friend, Captain [[Gower]]. The rascally scald beggarly lousy pragging knave Pistot—which you and yourself and all the world know to be no petter than a fellow, look you now, of no merits—he is come to me, and prings me pread and salt yesterday, look you, and bid me eat my leek. It was in a place where I could not breed no contention with him, but I will be so bold as to wear it in my cap till I see him once again, and then I will tell him a little piece of my desires. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Ensign [[Pistol]] |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Why, here a comes, swelling like a turkey-cock. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | ‘Tis no matter for his swellings nor his turkey-cocks. —God pless you Ensign [[Pistol]], you scurvy lousy knave, God pless you. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Ha, art thou bedlam? Dost thou thirst, base Trojan,<br>To have me fold up Parca’s fatal web?<br>Hence! I am qualmish at the smell of leek. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | I peseech you heartily, scurvy lousy knave, at my desires and my requests and my petitions, to eat, look you, this leek. Because, look you, you do not [[Love\|love]] it, nor your affections and your appetites and your digestions does not agree with it, I would [[Desire\|desire]] you to eat it. 26 |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Not for Cadwallader and all his goats. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | There is one goat for you. (He strikes [[Pistol]]) Will you be so good, scald knave, as eat it? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Base Trojan, thou shalt die. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | You say very true, scald knave, when God’s will is. I will [[Desire\|desire]] you to live in the mean [[Time\|time]], and eat your victuals. Come, there is sauce for it. (He strikes him) You called me yesterday ‘mountain-squire’, but I will make you today a ‘squire of low degree’. I pray you, fall to. If you can mock a leek you can eat a leek. |
| *(didascalia)* | He strikes him |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Enough, captain, you have astonished him. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | By Jesu, I will make him eat some part of my leek, or I will peat his pate four days and four nights.—Bite, I pray you. It is good for your green wound and your ploody coxcomb. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Must I bite? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Yes, certainly, and out of [[Doubt\|doubt]] and out of question too, and ambiguities. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | By this leek, I will most horribly [[Revenge\|revenge]]— |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Fluellen]] threatens him I eat and eat—I swear— |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Eat, I pray you. Will you have some more sauce to your leek? There is not enough leek to swear by. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Quiet thy cudgel, thou dost see I eat. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Much good do you, scald knave, heartily. Nay, pray you throw none away. The skin is good for your broken coxcomb. When you take occasions to see leeks hereafter, I pray you mock at ’em, that is all. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Good. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Ay, leeks is good. Hold you, there is a groat to heal your pate. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Me, a groat? |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | Yes, verity, and in [[Truth\|truth]] you shall take it, or I have another leek in my pocket which you shall eat. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | I take thy groat in earnest of [[Revenge\|revenge]]. |
| [[Fluellen\|FLUELLEN]] | If I owe you anything, I will pay you in cudgels. You shall be a woodmonger, and buy nothing of me but cudgels. God b’wi’ you, and keep you, and heal your pate. Exit |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | All hell shall stir for this. |
| [[Gower\|GOWER]] | Go, go, you are a counterfeit cowardly knave. Will you mock at an ancient tradition, begun upon an honourable respect and worn as a memorable trophy of predeceased valour, and dare not avouch in your deeds any of your words? I have seen you gleeking and galling at this gentleman twice or thrice. You thought, because he could not speak English in the native garb, he could not therefore handle an English cudgel. You find it otherwise. And henceforth let a Welsh correction teach you a good English condition. Fare ye [[Well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Doth Fortune play the hussy with me now?<br>News have I that my Nell is dead<br>I’th’ spital of a malady of France,<br>And there my rendezvous is quite cut off.<br>Old I do wax, and from my weary limbs<br>[[Honour]] is cudgelled. [[Well]], bawd I’ll turn,<br>And something lean to cutpurse of quick hand.<br>To England will I steal, and there I’ll steal,<br>And patches will I get unto these cudgelled scars,<br>And swear I got them in the Gallia wars. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter at one door [[King]] Harry, the Dukes of Exeter and [[Clarence]], the [[Earl of Warwick]], and other lords; at another, [[King]] Charles the Sixth of France, [[Queen Isabel]], the Duke of Burgundy, and other French, among them [[Princess Catherine]] and Alice |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Peace to this meeting, wherefor we are met.<br>Unto our brother France and to our sister,<br>Health and fair [[Time\|time]] of day. joy and good wishes<br>To our most fair and princely cousin Catherine;<br>And as a branch and member of this royalty,<br>By whom this great assembly is contrived,<br>We do salute you, Duke of Burgundy.<br>And princes French, and peers, health to you all. |
| [[King\|KING]] CHARLES | Right joyous are we to behold your face.<br>Most worthy brother England, fairly met.<br>So are you, princes English, every one. |
| [[Queen Isabel\|QUEEN ISABEL]] | So happy be the issue, brother England,<br>Of this good day and of this gracious meeting,<br>As we are now glad to behold your eyes—<br>Your eyes which hitherto have borne in them,<br>Against the French that met them in their bent,<br>The fatal balls of murdering basilisks.<br>The venom of such looks we fairly [[Hope\|hope]]<br>Have lost their quality, and that this day<br>Shall change all griefs and quarrels into [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | To cry amen to that, thus we appear. |
