| [[Queen Isabel\|QUEEN ISABEL]] | You English princes all, I do salute you. |
| BURGUNDY | My duty to you both, on equal [[Love\|love]],<br>Great Kings of France and England. That I have<br>laboured<br>With all my wits, my pains, and strong endeavours,<br>To bring your most imperial majesties<br>Unto this bar and royal interview,<br>Your mightiness on both parts best can witness.<br>Since, then, my office hath so far prevailed<br>That face to face and royal eye to eye<br>You have congreeted, let it not disgrace me<br>If I demand, before this royal view,<br>What rub or what impediment there is<br>Why that the naked, poor, and mangled peace,<br>Dear [[Nurse\|nurse]] of arts, plenties, and joyful births,<br>Should not in this best [[Garden\|garden]] of the world,<br>Our fertile France, put up her lovely visage?<br>Alas, she hath from France too long been chased,<br>And all her husbandry doth lie on heaps,<br>Corrupting in it own fertility.<br>Her vine, the merry cheerer of the heart,<br>Unprunèd dies; her hedges even-plashed<br>Like prisoners wildly overgrown with hair<br>Put forth disordered twigs; her fallow leas<br>The darnel, hemlock, and rank fumitory<br>Doth root upon, while that the coulter rusts<br>That should deracinate such savagery.<br>The even mead—that erst brought sweetly forth<br>The freckled cowslip, burnet, and green clover—<br>Wanting the scythe, all uncorrected, rank,<br>Conceives by idleness, and nothing teems<br>But hateful docks, rough thistles, kecksies, burs,<br>Losing both [[Beauty\|beauty]] and utility.<br>An all our vineyards, fallows, meads, and hedges,<br>Defective in their natures, grow to wildness,<br>Even so our houses and ourselves and children<br>Have lost, or do not learn for want of [[Time\|time]],<br>The sciences that should become our country,<br>But grow like savages—as soldiers will<br>That nothing do but meditate on [[Blood\|blood]]—<br>To swearing and stern looks, diffused attire,<br>And everything that seems unnatural.<br>Which to reduce into our former favour<br>You are assembled, and my speech entreats<br>That I may know the let why gentle peace<br>Should not expel these inconveniences<br>And bless us with her former qualities. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | If, Duke of Burgundy, you would the peace<br>Whose want gives growth to th’imperfections<br>Which you have cited, you must buy that peace<br>With full accord to all our just demands,<br>Whose tenors and particular effects<br>You have enscheduled briefly in your hands. |
| BURGUNDY | The [[King]] hath heard them, to the which as yet<br>There is no answer made. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | [[Well]] then, the peace,<br>Which you before so urged, lies in his answer. |
| [[King\|KING]] CHARLES | I have but with a cursitory eye<br>O’erglanced the articles. Pleaseth your grace<br>To appoint some of your council presently<br>To sit with us once more, with better heed<br>To re-survey them, we will suddenly<br>Pass our accept and peremptory answer. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Brother, we shall.—Go, Uncle Exeter<br>And brother [[Clarence]], and you, brother [[Gloucester]];<br>Warwick and Huntingdon, go with the [[King]],<br>And take with you free power to ratify,<br>Augment, or alter, as your wisdoms best<br>Shall see advantageable for our dignity,<br>Anything in or out of our demands,<br>And we’ll consign thereto.—Will you, fair sister,<br>Go with the princes, or stay here with us? |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | Our gracious brother, I will go with them.<br>Haply a woman’s voice may do some good<br>When articles too nicely urged be stood on. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Yet leave our cousin Catherine here with us.<br>She is our capital demand, comprised |
| *(didascalia)* | Within the fore-rank of our articles. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | She hath good leave. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[King]] Harry, Catherine, and Alice |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Fair Catherine, and most fair,<br>Will you vouchsafe to teach a soldier terms<br>Such as will enter at a lady’s ear<br>And plead his [[Love\|love]]-suit to her gentle heart? |
| CATHERINE | Your majesty shall mock at me. I cannot speak your England. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | O fair Catherine, if you will [[Love\|love]] me soundly with your French heart, I will be glad to hear you confess it brokenly with your English tongue. Do you like me, Kate? |
| CATHERINE | Pardonnez-moi, I cannot tell vat is ‘like me’. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | An angel is like you, Kate, and you are like an angel. |
| CATHERINE | (to Alice) Que dit-il?—que je suis semblable à les anges? |
| ALICE | Oui, vraiment—sauf votre grâce—ainsi dit-il. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I said so, dear Catherine, and I must not blush to affirm it. |
| CATHERINE | O bon Dieu! Les langues des hommes sont pleines de tromperies. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | What says she, fair one? That the tongues of men are full of deceits? |
| ALICE | Oui, dat de tongeus of de mans is be full of deceits—dat is de Princess. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | The Princess is the better Englishwoman. I‘[[Faith\|faith]], Kate, my wooing is fit for thy understanding. I am glad thou canst speak no better English, for if thou couldst, thou wouldst find me such a plain [[King\|king]] that thou wouldst think I had sold my farm to buy my [[Crown\|crown]]. I know no ways to mince it in [[Love\|love]], but directly to say, ‘I [[Love\|love]] you’; then if you urge me farther than to say, ‘Do you in [[Faith\|faith]]?’, I wear out my suit. Give me your answer, i’[[Faith\|faith]] do, and so clap hands and a bargain. How say you, lady? |
| CATHERINE | Sauf votre honneur, me understand [[Well\|well]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Marry, if you would put me to verses, or to [[Dance\|dance]] for your sake, Kate, why, you undid me. For the one I have neither words nor measure, and for the other I have no strength in measure—yet a reasonable measure in strength. If I could win a lady at leap-frog, or by vaulting into my saddle with my armour on my back, under the correction of bragging be it spoken, I should quickly leap into a wife. Or if I might buffet for my [[Love\|love]], or bound my [[Horse\|horse]] for her favours, I could lay on like a butcher, and sit like a jackanapes, never off. But before God, Kate, I cannot look greenly, nor gasp out my eloquence, nor I have no cunning in protestation—only downright oaths, which I never use till urged, nor never break for urging. If thou canst [[Love\|love]] a fellow of this temper, Kate, whose face is not worth sunburning, that never looks in his glass for [[Love\|love]] of anything he sees there, let thine eye be thy cook. I speak to thee plain soldier: if thou canst [[Love\|love]] me for this, take me. If not, to say to thee that I shall die, is true—but for thy [[Love\|love]], by the Lord, no. Yet I [[Love\|love]] thee, too. And while thou livest, dear Kate, take a fellow of plain and uncoined constancy, for he perforce must do thee right, because he hath not the gift to woo in other places. For these fellows of infinite tongue, that can rhyme themselves into ladies’ favours, they do always reason themselves out again. What! A speaker is but a prater, a rhyme is but a ballad; a good leg will fall, a straight back will stoop, a black beard will turn white, a curled pate will grow bald, a fair face will wither, a full eye will wax hollow, but a good heart, Kate, is the sun and the [[Moon\|moon]]—or rather the sun and not the [[Moon\|moon]], for it shines bright and never changes, but keeps his course truly. If thou would have such a one, take me; and take me, take a soldier; take a soldier, take a [[King\|king]]. And what sayst thou then to my [[Love\|love]]? Speak, my fair—and fairly, I pray thee. |
| CATHERINE | Is it possible dat I sould [[Love\|love]] de ennemi of France? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | No, it is not possible you should [[Love\|love]] the enemy of France, Kate. But in loving me, you should [[Love\|love]] the friend of France, for I [[Love\|love]] France so [[Well\|well]] that I will not part with a village of it, I will have it all mine; and Kate, when France is mine, and I am yours, then yours is France, and you are mine. |
| CATHERINE | I cannot tell vat is dat. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | No, Kate? I will tell thee in French—which I am sure will hang upon my tongue like a new-married wife about her husband’s neck, hardly to be shook off. le quand suis le possesseur de France, et quand vous avez le possession de moi—let me see, what then? Saint Denis be my [[Speed\|speed]]!—donc vôtre est France, et vous êtes mienne. It is as easy for me, Kate, to conquer the kingdom as to speak so much more French. I shall never move thee in French, unless it be to laugh at me. |
| CATHERINE | Sauf votre honneur, le français que vous parlez, il est meilleur que l’anglais lequel je parle. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | No, [[Faith\|faith]], is’t not, Kate. But thy speaking of my tongue, and I thine, most truly-falsely, must needs be granted to be much at one. But Kate, dost thou understand thus much English? Canst thou [[Love\|love]] me? |
| CATHERINE | I cannot tell. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Can any of your neighbours tell, Kate? I’ll ask them. Come, I know thou lovest me, and at [[Night\|night]] when you come into your closet you’ll question this gentlewoman about me, and I know, Kate, you will to her dispraise those parts in me that you [[Love\|love]] with your heart. But good Kate, mock me mercifully—the rather, gentle princess, because I [[Love\|love]] thee cruelly. If ever thou be’st mine, Kate—as I have a saving [[Faith\|faith]] within me tells me thou shalt—I get thee with scrambling, and thou must therefore needs prove a good soldier-breeder. Shall not thou and I, between Saint Denis and Saint George, compound a boy, half-French half-English, that shall go to Constantinople and take the Turk by the beard? Shall we not? What sayst thou, my fair flowerde-luce? |
| CATHERINE | I do not know dat. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | No, ’tis hereafter to know, but now to promise. Do but now promise, Kate, you will endeavour for your French part of such a boy, and for my English moiety take the word of a [[King\|king]] and a bachelor. How answer you, la plus belle Catherine du monde, mon très chere et divine deésse? |
| CATHERINE | Your majesté ’ave faux French enough to deceive de most sage demoiselle dat is en France. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Now fie upon my false French! By mine [[Honour\|honour]], in true English, I [[Love\|love]] thee, Kate. By which [[Honour\|honour]] I dare not swear thou lovest me, yet my [[Blood\|blood]] begins to flatter me that thou dost, notwithstanding the poor and untempering effect of my visage. Now beshrew my father’s [[Ambition\|ambition]]! He was thinking of civil wars when he got me; therefore was I created with a stubborn outside, with an aspect of iron, that when I come to woo ladies I fright them. But in [[Faith\|faith]], Kate, the elder I wax the better I shall appear. My comfort is that old age, that ill layer-up of [[Beauty\|beauty]], can do no more spoil upon my face. Thou hast me, if thou hast me, at the worst, and thou shalt wear me, if thou wear me, better and better; and therefore tell me, most fair Catherine, will you have me? Put off your maiden blushes, avouch the thoughts of your heart with the looks of an empress, take me by the hand and say, ‘Harry of England, I am thine’—which word thou shalt no sooner bless mine ear withal, but I will tell thee aloud, ‘England is thine, Ireland is thine, France is thine, and Henry Plantagenet is thine’—who, though I speak it before his face, if he be not fellow with the best [[King\|king]], thou shalt find the best [[King\|king]] of good fellows. Come, your answer in broken music—for thy voice is music and thy English broken. Therefore, [[Queen\|queen]] of all, Catherine, break thy mind to me in broken English: wilt thou have me? |
| CATHERINE | Dat is as it shall please de roi mon père. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Nay, it will please him [[Well\|well]], Kate. It shall please him, Kate. |
| CATHERINE | Den it sail also content me. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Upon that I kiss your hand, and I call you my [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| CATHERINE | Laissez, mon seigneur, laissez, laissez! Ma foi, je ne veux point que vous abbaissez votre grandeur en baisant la main d’une de votre seigneurie indigne serviteur. Excusezmoi, je vous supplie, mon treis-puissant seigneur. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Then I will kiss your lips, Kate. 255 |
| CATHERINE | Les dames et demoiselles pour être baisées devant leurs noces, il n’est pas la coutume de France. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | (to Alice) Madam my interpreter, what says she? |
| ALICE | Dat it is not be de façon pour les ladies of France—I cannot tell vat is baiser en Anglish. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | To kiss. |
| ALICE | Your majesté entend bettre que moi. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | It is not a fashion for the maids in France to kiss before they are married, would she say? 265 ALICE Oui, vraiment. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | O Kate, nice customs curtsy to great kings. Dear Kate, you and I cannot be confined within the weak list of a country’s fashion. We are the makers of manners, Kate, and the liberty that follows our places stops the mouth of all find-faults, as I will do yours, for upholding the nice fashion of your country in denying me a kiss. Therefore, patiently and yielding. (He kisses her) You have witchcraft in your lips, Kate. There is more eloquence in a sugar touch of them than in the tongues of the French Council, and they should sooner persuade Harry of England than a general petition of monarchs. Here comes your father Enter [[King]] Charles, [[Queen Isabel]], the Duke of Burgundy, and the French and English lords |
| BURGUNDY | God save your majesty. My royal cousin, teach you our princess English? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I would have her learn, my fair cousin, how perfectly I [[Love\|love]] her, and that is good English. |
| BURGUNDY | Is she not apt? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Our tongue is rough, coz, and my condition is not smooth, so that having neither the voice nor the heart of flattery about me I cannot so conjure up the spirit of [[Love\|love]] in her that he will appear in his true likeness. |
| BURGUNDY | Pardon the frankness of my mirth, if I answer you for that. If you would conjure in her, you must make a circle; if conjure up [[Love\|love]] in her in his true likeness, he must appear naked and blind. Can you blame her then, being a maid yet rosed over with the virgin crimson of modesty, if she deny the appearance of a naked blind boy in her naked seeing [[Self\|self]]? It were, my lord, a hard condition for a maid to consign to. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Yet they do wink and yield, as [[Love\|love]] is blind and enforces. |
| BURGUNDY | They are then excused, my lord, when they see not what they do. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Then, good my lord, teach your cousin to consent winking. |
| BURGUNDY | I will wink on her to consent, my lord, if you will teach her to know my meaning. For maids, [[Well\|well]] summered and warm kept, are like flies at Bartholomew-tide: blind, though they have their eyes. And then they will endure handling, which before would not abide looking on. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | This moral ties me over to [[Time\|time]] and a hot [[Summer\|summer]], and so I shall catch the fly, your cousin, in the latter end, and she must be blind too. |
| BURGUNDY | As [[Love\|love]] is, my lord, before that it loves. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | It is so. And you may, some of you, thank [[Love\|love]] for my blindness, who cannot see many a fair French [[City\|city]] for one fair French maid that stands in my way. |
| [[King\|KING]] CHARLES | Yes, my lord, you see them perspectively, the cities turned into a maid—for they are all girdled with maiden walls that war hath never entered. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Shall Kate be my wife? |
| [[King\|KING]] CHARLES | So please you. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I am content, so the maiden cities you talk of may wait on her: so the maid that stood in the way for my wish shall show me the way to my will. |
| [[King\|KING]] CHARLES | We have consented to all terms of reason. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Is’t so, my lords of England? |
| *(didascalia)* | WARWICKI |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | The [[King]] hath granted every article:<br>His daughter first, and so in sequel all,<br>According to their firm proposed natures. |
| EXETER | Only he hath not yet subscribed this:<br>where your majesty demands that the [[King of France]],<br>having any occasion to write for matter of grant, shall<br>name your highness in this form and with this addition: |
| *(didascalia)* | reads in French, Notre très |
| EXETER | cher fils Henri, Roi<br><br>d’Angleterre, Heritier de France, and thus in Latin,<br>Praeclarissimus filius noster Henricus, Rex Angliae et<br><br>Haeres Franciae. |
| [[King\|KING]] CHARLES | Nor this I have not, brother, so denied,<br>But your request shall make me let it pass. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I pray you then, in [[Love\|love]] and dear alliance,<br>Let that one article rank with the rest,<br>And thereupon give me your daughter. |
| [[King\|KING]] CHARLES | Take her, fair son, and from her [[Blood\|blood]] raise up<br>Issue to me, that the contending kingdoms<br>Of France and England, whose very shores look pale<br>With envy of each other’s happiness,<br>May cease their hatred, and this dear conjunction<br>Plant neighbourhood and Christian-like accord<br>In their sweet bosoms, that never war advance<br>His bleeding [[Sword\|sword]] ’twixt England and fair France. |
| *(didascalia)* | ALL Amen. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Now welcome, Kate, and bear me witness all<br>That here I kiss her as my sovereign [[Queen]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish |
| [[Queen Isabel\|QUEEN ISABEL]] | God, the best maker of all marriages,<br>Combine your hearts in one, your realms in one.<br>As man and wife, being two, are one in [[Love\|love]],<br>So be there ‘twixt your kingdoms such a spousal<br>That never may ill office or fell [[Jealousy\|jealousy]],<br>Which troubles oft the bed of blessed [[Marriage\|marriage]],<br>Thrust in between the paction of these kingdoms<br>To make [[divorce]] of their incorporate league;<br>That English may as French, French Englishmen,<br>Receive each other, God speak this ‘Amen’. |
| ALL | Amen. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Prepare we for our [[Marriage\|marriage]]. On which day,<br>My lord of Burgundy, we’ll take your oath,<br>And all the peers‘, for surety of our leagues.<br>Then shall I swear to Kate, and you to me,<br>And may our oaths [[Well\|well]] kept and prosp’rous be. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sennet. Exeunt |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Epilogue |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Chorus]] |
| [[Chorus\|CHORUS]] | Thus far with rough and all-unable pen<br>Our bending author hath pursued the story,<br>In little room confining mighty men,<br>Mangling by starts the full course of their glory.<br>Small [[Time\|time]], but in that small most greatly lived<br>This star of England. Fortune made his [[Sword\|sword]],<br>By which the world’s best [[Garden\|garden]] he achieved, And of it left his son imperial lord.<br>Henry the Sixth, in infant bands crowned [[King of France\|king Of France]] and England, did this [[King\|king]] succeed,<br>Whose state so many had the managing That they lost France and made his England bleed,<br>Which oft our stage hath shown—and, for their sake,<br>In your fair minds let this acceptance take. Exit |
| ADDITIONAL PASSAGES | The [[Dauphin]]/Bourbon variant, which usually involves only the alteration of speech-prefixes, has several consequences for the dialogue and structure of 4.5. There follow edited texts of the Folio and Quarto versions of this scene. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Constable, Orleans, Bourbon, the [[Dauphin]], and Rambures |
| CONSTABLE | O diable!<br>ORLÉANS O Seigneur! Le jour est perdu, tout est perdu. |
| [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | Mort de ma vie! All is confounded, all.<br>Reproach and everlasting shame<br>Sits mocking in our plumes.<br>A short alarum<br><br>O méchante fortune! Do not run away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Rambures |
| CONSTABLE | Why, all our ranks are broke. |
| [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | O perdurable shame! Let’s stab ourselves:<br>Be these the wretches that we played at dice for?<br>ORLÉANS<br>Is this the [[King\|king]] we sent to for his ransom? |
| BOURBON | Shame, an eternall shame, nothing but shame!<br>Let us die in [[Pride\|pride]]. In once more, back again!<br>And he that will not follow Bourbon now,<br>Let him go [[Home\|home]], and with his cap in hand<br>Like a base leno hold the chamber door,<br>Whilst by a slave no gentler than my dog<br>His fairest daughter is contaminated. |
| CONSTABLE | Disorder that hath spoiled us, friend us now,<br>Let us on heaps go offer up our lives.<br>ORLÉANS<br>We are enough yet living in the field<br>To smother up the English in our throngs,<br>If any order might be thought upon. |
| BOURBON | The devil take order now. I’ll to the throng.<br>Let life be short, else shame will be too long. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the four French lords: the Constable, Orléans, Bourbon, and Gebon |
| GEBON | O diabello! |
| CONSTABLE | Mort de ma vie!<br>ORLÉANS O what a day is this! |
| BOURBON | O jour de honte, all is gone, all is lost. |
| CONSTABLE | We are enough yet living in the field<br>To smother up the English,<br>If any order might be thought upon. |
| BOURBON | A plague of order! Once more to the field!<br>And he that will not follow Bourbon now,<br>Let him go [[Home\|home]], and with his cap in hand,<br>Like a base leno hold the chamber door,<br>Whilst by a slave no gentler than my dog<br>His fairest daughter is contaminated. |
| CONSTABLE | Disorder that hath spoiled us, right us now.<br>Come we in heaps, we’ll offer up our lives<br>Unto these English, or else die with fame. |
| *(didascalia)* | BOURBON Come, come along. |
| CONSTABLE | Let’s die with [[Honour\|honour]], our shame doth last too long. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_1" data-title="Henry V — Act I, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/king,archetype/lover,motif/clock,motif/crown,motif/desire,motif/night,motif/summer,motif/time,motif/well,concept/body,concept/doubt,concept/honour,concept/self,concept/wonder,histref/reformation,character/henry_v"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Archbishop of Canterbury and the Bishop of Ely |
| CANTERBURY | My lord, I’ll tell you. That [[Self\|self]] bill is urged<br>Which in th’eleventh year of the last [[King\|king]]’s reign<br>Was like, and had indeed against us passed,<br>But that the scrambling and unquiet [[Time\|time]]<br>Did push it out of farther question. |
| ELY | But how, my lord, shall we resist it now? |
| CANTERBURY | It must be thought on. If it pass against us,<br>We lose the better half of our possession,<br>For all the temporal lands which men devout<br>By testament have given to the Church<br>Would they strip from us—being valued thus:<br>As much as would maintain, to the [[King]]’s [[Honour\|honour]],<br>Full fifteen earls and fifteen hundred knights,<br>Six thousand and two hundred good esquires;<br>And, to relief of lazars and weak age,<br>Of indigent faint souls past corporal toil,<br>A hundred almshouses right [[Well\|well]] supplied;<br>And to the coffers of the [[King]] beside<br>A thousand pounds by th’ year. Thus runs the bill. |
| ELY | This would drink deep. |
| CANTERBURY | ’Twould drink the cup and all. |
| ELY | But what prevention? |
| CANTERBURY | The [[King]] is full of grace and fair regard. |
| ELY | And a true [[Lover\|lover]] of the holy Church. |
| CANTERBURY | The courses of his youth promised it not.<br>The breath no sooner left his father’s [[Body\|body]]<br>But that his wildness, mortified in him,<br>Seemed to die too. Yea, at that very moment<br>Consideration like an angel came<br>And whipped th‘offending Adam out of him,<br>Leaving his [[Body\|body]] as a paradise<br>T’envelop and contain celestial spirits.<br>Never was such a sudden scholar made;<br>Never came [[Reformation\|reformation]] in a flood<br>With such a heady currance scouring faults;<br>Nor never Hydra-headed wilfulness<br>So soon did lose his seat—and all at once—<br>As in this [[King\|king]]. |
| ELY | We are blessed in the change. |
| CANTERBURY | Hear him but reason in divinity<br>And, all-admiring, with an inward wish<br>You would [[Desire\|desire]] the [[King]] were made a prelate;<br>Hear him [[debate]] of commonwealth affairs,<br>You would say it hath been all-in-all his study;<br>List his discourse of war, and you shall hear<br>A fearful battle rendered you in music;<br>Turn him to any cause of policy,<br>The Gordian knot of it he will unloose,<br>Familiar as his garter—that when he speaks,<br>The air, a chartered libertine, is still,<br>And the mute [[Wonder\|wonder]] lurketh in men’s ears<br>To steal his sweet and honeyed sentences:<br>So that the art and practic part of life<br>Must be the mistress to this theoric.<br>Which is a [[Wonder\|wonder]] how his grace should glean it,<br>Since his addiction was to courses vain,<br>His companies unlettered, rude, and shallow,<br>His hours filled up with riots, banquets, sports,<br>And never noted in him any study,<br>Any retirement, any sequestration<br>From open haunts and popularity. |
| ELY | The strawberry grows underneath the nettle,<br>And wholesome berries thrive and ripen best<br>Neighboured by fruit of baser quality;<br>And so the Prince obscured his contemplation<br>Under the veil of wildness—which, no [[Doubt\|doubt]],<br>Grew like the [[Summer\|summer]] grass, fastest by [[Night\|night]],<br>Unseen, yet crescive in his faculty. |
| CANTERBURY | It must be so, for miracles are ceased,<br>And therefore we must needs admit the means<br>How things are perfected. |
| ELY | But, my good lord,<br>How now for mitigation of this bill<br>Urged by the Commons? Doth his majesty<br>Incline to it, or no? |
| CANTERBURY | He seems indifferent,<br>Or rather swaying more upon our part<br>Than cherishing th’exhibitors against us;<br>For I have made an offer to his majesty,<br>Upon our spiritual convocation<br>And in regard of causes now in hand,<br>Which I have opened to his grace at large:<br>As touching France, to give a greater sum<br>Than ever at one [[Time\|time]] the clergy yet<br>Did to his predecessors part withal. |
| ELY | How did this offer seem received, my lord? |
| CANTERBURY | With good acceptance of his majesty,<br>Save that there was not [[Time\|time]] enough to hear,<br>As I perceived his grace would fain have done,<br>The severals and unhidden passages<br>Of his true titles to some certain dukedoms,<br>And generally to the [[Crown\|crown]] and seat of France,<br>Derived from Edward, his great-grandfather. |
| ELY | What was th’impediment that broke this off? |
| CANTERBURY | The French ambassador upon that instant<br>Craved audience—and the hour I think is come<br>To give him hearing. Is it four o’[[Clock\|clock]]? |
| ELY | It is. 95 |
| CANTERBURY | Then go we in, to know his embassy—<br>Which I could with a ready guess declare<br>Before the Frenchman speak a word of it. |
| ELY | I’ll wait upon you, and I long to hear it. Exeunt |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano l’Arcivescovo di Canterbury e il Vescovo di Ely |
| CANTERBURY | Mio signore, ve lo dirò. Quel [[Self|medesimo]] decreto si ripropone<br>che nell’undicesimo anno del regno dell’ultimo [[King|re]]<br>stava per passare, e in verità passò contro di noi,<br>se non che il tumultuoso e inquieto [[Time|tempo]]<br>lo respinse fuori da ogni ulteriore dibattito. |
| ELY | Ma come, mio signore, potremo resistervi ora? |
| CANTERBURY | Bisogna pensarci. Se passa contro di noi,<br>perdiamo la miglior metà dei nostri beni,<br>ché tutte le terre temporali che uomini devoti<br>per testamento hanno donato alla Chiesa<br>ci verrebbero strappate—e sono stimate così:<br>quanto basterebbe a mantenere, ad [[Honour|onore]] del [[King|Re]],<br>ben quindici conti e millecinquecento cavalieri,<br>seimiladuecento buoni scudieri;<br>e, a sollievo dei lebbrosi e della debole vecchiaia,<br>di anime indigenti e fiacche, incapaci d’ogni fatica del corpo,<br>cento ospizi assai [[Well|ben]] provveduti;<br>e alle casse del [[King|Re]] per giunta<br>mille sterline all’anno. Così recita il decreto. |
| ELY | Sarebbe un lungo sorso. |
| CANTERBURY | Berrebbe la coppa e tutto quanto. |
| ELY | Ma quale rimedio? |
| CANTERBURY | Il [[King|Re]] è pieno di grazia e di giusto riguardo. |
| ELY | E vero [[Lover|amante]] della santa Chiesa. |
| CANTERBURY | Il corso della sua giovinezza non lo prometteva.<br>Non appena il respiro lasciò il [[Body|corpo]] di suo padre,<br>la sua sfrenatezza, mortificata in lui,<br>parve morire anch’essa. Sì, in quel medesimo istante<br>la Riflessione venne come un angelo<br>e flagellò fuori di lui l’Adamo peccatore,<br>lasciando il suo [[Body|corpo]] come un paradiso<br>ad avvolgere e contenere spiriti celesti.<br>Mai si fece dotto uno così d’un tratto;<br>mai venne [[Reformation|riforma]] in tale piena<br>con corrente sì impetuosa a lavare le colpe;<br>né mai ostinazione dalle teste d’Idra<br>perdette così presto il proprio seggio—e tutto in una volta—<br>come in questo [[King|re]]. |
| ELY | Siamo benedetti nel mutamento. |
| CANTERBURY | Udite solo il suo ragionare di teologia<br>e, pieni d’ammirazione, con intimo voto<br>[[Desire|desidereresti]] che il [[King|Re]] fosse fatto prelato;<br>uditelo [[debate|discutere]] d’affari di stato,<br>direste che sono stati il suo unico studio;<br>ascoltate il suo discorso di guerra, e udirete<br>una tremenda battaglia resa in musica;<br>volgetelo a qualsiasi questione di governo,<br>e ne scioglierà il nodo gordiano,<br>familiare come la sua giarrettiera—sicché quando parla<br>l’aria, libertina senza legge, si fa immobile,<br>e la muta [[Wonder|meraviglia]] s’annida negli orecchi degli uomini<br>per rubare le sue dolci e mielate sentenze:<br>tanto che l’arte e la parte pratica della vita<br>dev’essere maestra a questa teoria.<br>Il che è una [[Wonder|meraviglia]]: come mai sua grazia l’abbia colta,<br>poiché la sua inclinazione era a vane occupazioni,<br>le sue compagnie illetterate, rozze e superficiali,<br>le sue ore colme di baldorie, banchetti, giochi,<br>e mai gli si notò studio alcuno,<br>alcun ritiro, alcuna separazione<br>dai luoghi aperti e dalla folla. |
| ELY | La fragola cresce sotto l’ortica,<br>e le bacche salubri prosperano e maturano meglio<br>vicine a frutti di più bassa qualità;<br>e così il Principe oscurò la sua contemplazione<br>sotto il velo della sfrenatezza—la quale, senza [[Doubt|dubbio]],<br>crebbe come l’erba d’[[Summer|estate]], più rapida di [[Night|notte]],<br>inosservata, eppure crescente nella sua virtù. |
| CANTERBURY | Dev’essere così, ché i miracoli sono cessati,<br>e perciò ci tocca ammettere i mezzi<br>per cui le cose si compiono. |
| ELY | Ma, mio buon signore,<br>che ne è ora della mitigazione di questo decreto<br>sollecitato dai Comuni? Sua maestà<br>vi propende, o no? |
| CANTERBURY | Sembra indifferente,<br>o piuttosto piegare più dalla nostra parte<br>che favorire i proponenti contro di noi;<br>ché io ho fatto un’offerta a sua maestà,<br>in nome della nostra convocazione spirituale<br>e in riguardo alle cause ora in corso,<br>che ho esposto ampiamente a sua grazia:<br>quanto alla Francia, di dare una somma maggiore<br>di quanta mai in una sola [[Time|volta]] il clero finora<br>abbia ceduto ai suoi predecessori. |
| ELY | Come parve accolta quest’offerta, mio signore? |
| CANTERBURY | Con buon gradimento di sua maestà,<br>se non che non vi fu [[Time|tempo]] bastante per udire,<br>come mi accorsi che sua grazia avrebbe volentieri fatto,<br>i particolari e i passaggi manifesti<br>dei suoi veri titoli a certi ducati,<br>e in generale alla [[Crown|corona]] e al seggio di Francia,<br>derivati da Edoardo, suo bisavolo. |
| ELY | Quale fu l’impedimento che troncò tutto questo? |
| CANTERBURY | L’ambasciatore di Francia in quell’istante<br>chiese udienza—e l’ora credo sia giunta<br>di dargli ascolto. Sono le quattro all’[[Clock|orologio]]? |
| ELY | Lo sono. 95 |
| CANTERBURY | Andiamo dunque dentro, a conoscere la sua ambasciata—<br>che io potrei con pronta congettura dichiarare<br>prima che il Francese ne dica una parola. |
| ELY | Vi accompagnerò, e ardo dal desiderio di udirla. Escono |



<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_2" data-title="Henry V — Act I, Scene 2" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/king,archetype/tyrant,archetype/usurper,motif/bees,motif/blood,motif/crown,motif/fire,motif/flag,motif/grave,motif/hill,motif/home,motif/lion,motif/marriage,motif/nature,motif/river,motif/soul,motif/summer,motif/time,motif/well,concept/conscience,concept/courage,concept/fear,concept/greatness,concept/history,concept/hope,concept/mortality,concept/nature,concept/pride,concept/truth,form/history,form/tragedy,setting/germany,character/bottom,character/clarence,character/dauphin,character/edward_the_black_prince,character/gloucester,character/henry_v,character/queen_isabel"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Harry, the Dukes of [[Gloucester]], [[Clarence]], and Exeter, and the Earls of Warwick and Westmorland |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Where is my gracious lord of Canterbury? |
| EXETER | Not here in presence. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Send for him, good uncle. |
| WESTMORLAND | Shall we call in th’ambassador, my liege? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Not yet, my cousin. We would be resolved,<br>Before we hear him, of some things of weight<br>That task our thoughts, concerning us and France. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Archbishop of Canterbury and the Bishop of Ely |
| CANTERBURY | God and his angels guard your sacred throne,<br>And make you long become it. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Sure we thank you.<br>My learnèd lord, we pray you to proceed,<br>And justly and religiously unfold<br>Why the law Salic that they have in France<br>Or should or should not bar us in our claim.<br>And God forbid, my dear and faithful lord,<br>That you should fashion, wrest, or bow your reading,<br>Or nicely charge your understanding [[Soul\|soul]]<br>With opening titles miscreate, whose right<br>Suits not in native colours with the [[Truth\|truth]];<br>For God doth know how many now in health<br>Shall drop their [[Blood\|blood]] in approbation<br>Of what your reverence shall incite us to.<br>Therefore take heed how you impawn our person,<br>How you awake our sleeping [[Sword\|sword]] of war;<br>We charge you in the name of God take heed.<br>For never two such kingdoms did contend<br>Without much fall of [[Blood\|blood]], whose guiltless drops<br>Are every one a woe, a sore complaint<br>’Gainst him whose wrongs gives edge unto the swords<br>That makes such waste in brief [[Mortality\|mortality]].<br>Under this conjuration speak, my lord,<br>For we will hear, note, and believe in heart<br>That what you speak is in your [[Conscience\|conscience]] washed<br>As pure as sin with baptism. |
| CANTERBURY | Then hear me, gracious sovereign, and you peers<br>That owe your selves, your lives, and services<br>To this imperial throne. There is no bar<br>To make against your highness’ claim to France<br>But this, which they produce from Pharamond:<br>‘In terram Salicam mulieres ne succedant’—<br><br>’No woman shall succeed in Salic land’—<br>Which ‘Salic land’ the French unjustly gloss<br>To be the realm of France, and Pharamond<br>The founder of this law and female bar.<br>Yet their own authors faithfully affirm<br>That the land Salic is in [[Germany]],<br>Between the floods of Saale and of Elbe,<br>Where, Charles the Great having subdued the Saxons,<br>There left behind and settled certain French<br>Who, holding in disdain the German women<br>For some dishonest manners of their life,<br>Established there this law: to wit, no female<br>Should be inheritrix in Salic land—<br>Which Salic, as I said, ’twixt Elbe and Saale,<br>Is at this day in [[Germany]] called Meissen.<br>Then doth it [[Well\|well]] appear the Salic Law<br>Was not devised for the realm of France.<br>Nor did the French possess the Salic land<br>Until four hundred one-and-twenty years<br>After defunction of [[King]] Pharamond,<br>Idly supposed the founder of this law,<br>Who died within the year of our redemption<br>Four hundred twenty-six; and Charles the Great<br>Subdued the Saxons, and did seat the French<br>Beyond the [[River\|river]] Saale, in the year<br>Eight hundred five. Besides, their writers say,<br>[[King]] Pépin, which deposed Childéric,<br>Did, as heir general—being descended<br>Of Blithild, which was daughter to [[King]] Clotaire—<br>Make claim and title to the [[Crown\|crown]] of France.<br>Hugh Capet also—who usurped the [[Crown\|crown]]<br>Of Charles the Duke of Lorraine, sole heir male<br>Of the true line and stock of Charles the Great—<br>To fine his title with some shows of [[Truth\|truth]],<br>Though in pure [[Truth\|truth]] it was corrupt and naught,<br>Conveyed himself as heir to th’ Lady Lingard,<br>Daughter to Charlemain, who was the son<br>To Louis the Emperor, and Louis the son<br>Of Charles the Great. Also, [[King]] Louis the Ninth,<br>Who was sole heir to the [[Usurper\|usurper]] Capet,<br>Could not keep quiet in his [[Conscience\|conscience]],<br>Wearing the [[Crown\|crown]] of France, till satisfied<br>That fair [[Queen Isabel]], his grandmother,<br>Was lineal of the Lady Ermengarde,<br>Daughter to Charles, the foresaid Duke of Lorraine;<br>By the which [[Marriage\|marriage]], the line of Charles the Great<br>Was reunited to the [[Crown\|crown]] of France.<br>So that, as clear as is the [[Summer\|summer]]’s sun,<br>[[King]] Pépin’s title and Hugh Capet’s claim,<br>[[King]] Louis his satisfaction, all appear<br>To hold in right and title of the female;<br>So do the kings of France unto this day,<br>Howbeit they would hold up this Salic Law<br>To bar your highness claiming from the female,<br>And rather choose to hide them in a net<br>Than amply to embar their crooked titles,<br>Usurped from you and your progenitors. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | May I with right and [[Conscience\|conscience]] make this claim? |
| CANTERBURY | The sin upon my head, dread sovereign.<br>For in the Book of Numbers is it writ,<br>‘When the son dies, let the inheritance<br>Descend unto the daughter.’ Gracious lord,<br>Stand for your own; unwind your bloody [[Flag\|flag]];<br>Look back into your mighty ancestors.<br>Go, my dread lord, to your great-grandsire’s tomb,<br>From whom you claim; invoke his warlike spirit,<br>And your great-uncle’s, [[Edward the Black Prince]],<br>Who on the French ground played a [[Tragedy\|tragedy]],<br>Making defeat on the full power of France,<br>Whiles his most mighty father on a [[Hill\|hill]]<br>Stood smiling to behold his [[Lion\|lion]]’s whelp<br>Forage in [[Blood\|blood]] of French nobility. no<br>O noble English, that could entertain<br>With half their forces the full [[Pride\|pride]] of France,<br>And let another half stand laughing by,<br>All out of work, and cold for action. |
| ELY | Awake remembrance of those valiant dead,<br>And with your puissant arm renew their feats.<br>You are their heir, you sit upon their throne,<br>The [[Blood\|blood]] and [[Courage\|courage]] that renowned them<br>Runs in your veins—and my thrice-puissant liege<br>Is in the very May-morn of his youth,<br>Ripe for exploits and mighty enterprises. |
| EXETER | Your brother kings and monarchs of the earth<br>Do all expect that you should rouse yourself<br>As did the former lions of your [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| WESTMORLAND | They know your grace hath cause; and means and<br>might,<br>So hath your highness. Never [[King\|king]] of England<br>Had nobles richer and more loyal subjects,<br>Whose hearts have left their bodies here in England<br>And lie pavilioned in the fields of France. |
| CANTERBURY | O let their bodies follow, my dear liege,<br>With [[Blood\|blood]] and [[Sword\|sword]] and [[Fire\|fire]], to win your right.<br>In aid whereof, we of the spiritualty<br>Will raise your highness such a mighty sum<br>As never did the clergy at one [[Time\|time]]<br>Bring in to any of your ancestors. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | We must not only arm t’invade the French,<br>But lay down our proportions to defend<br>Against the Scot, who will make raid upon us<br>With all advantages. |
| CANTERBURY | They of those marches, gracious sovereign,<br>Shall be a wall sufficient to defend<br>Our inland from the pilfering borderers. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | We do not mean the coursing snatchers only,<br>But [[Fear\|fear]] the main intendment of the Scot,<br>Who hath been still a giddy neighbour to us.<br>For you shall read that my great-grandfather<br>Never unmasked his power unto France<br>But that the Scot on his unfurnished kingdom<br>Came pouring like the tide into a breach<br>With ample and brim fullness of his force<br>Galling the gleaned land with hot assays,<br>Girding with grievous siege castles and towns,<br>That England, being empty of defence,<br>Hath shook and trembled at the bruit thereof. |
| CANTERBURY | She hath been then more feared than harmed, my<br>liege. 155<br>For hear her but exampled by herself:<br>When all her chivalry hath been in France<br>And she a mourning widow of her nobles,<br>She hath herself not only [[Well\|well]] defended<br>But taken and impounded as a stray<br>The [[King]] of Scots, whom she did send to France<br>To fill [[King]] Edward’s fame with prisoner kings<br>And make your chronicle as rich with praise<br>As is the ooze and [[Bottom\|bottom]] of the sea<br>With sunken wrack and sumless treasuries. |
| *(didascalia)* | A LORD |
| CANTERBURY | But there’s a saying very old and true:<br>‘If that you will France win, Then with Scotland first begin.’<br>For once the eagle England being in prey,<br>To her unguarded nest the weasel Scot<br>Comes sneaking, and so sucks her princely eggs,<br>Playing the mouse in absence of the cat,<br>To ’tame and havoc more than she can eat. |
| EXETER | It follows then the cat must stay at [[Home\|home]].<br>Yet that is but a crushed necessity,<br>Since we have locks to safeguard necessaries<br>And pretty traps to catch the petty thieves.<br>While that the armed hand doth fight abroad,<br>Th’advisèd head defends itself at [[Home\|home]].<br>For [[government]], though high and low and lower,<br>Put into parts, doth keep in one consent,<br>Congreeing in a full and natural close,<br>Like music. |
| CANTERBURY | True. Therefore doth heaven divide<br>The state of man in divers functions,<br>Setting endeavour in continual motion;<br>To which is fixed, as an aim or butt,<br>Obedience. For so work the honey-[[Bees\|bees]],<br>Creatures that by a rule in [[Nature\|nature]] teach<br>The act of order to a peopled kingdom.<br>They have a [[King\|king]], and officers of sorts,<br>Where some like magistrates correct at [[Home\|home]];<br>Others like merchants venture trade abroad;<br>Others like soldiers, armed in their stings,<br>Make boot upon the [[Summer\|summer]]’s velvet buds,<br>Which pillage they with merry march bring [[Home\|home]]<br>To the tent royal of their emperor,<br>Who busied in his majesty surveys<br>The singing masons building roofs of gold,<br>The civil citizens lading up the honey,<br>The poor mechanic porters crowding in<br>Their heavy burdens at his narrow gate,<br>The sad-eyed justice with his surly hum<br>Delivering o’er to executors pale<br>The lazy yawning drone. I this infer:<br>That many things, having full reference<br>To one consent, may work contrariously.<br>As many arrows, loosed several ways,<br>Fly to one mark, as many ways meet in one town,<br>As many fresh streams meet in one salt sea,<br>As many lines close in the dial’s centre,<br>So may a thousand actions once afoot<br>End in one purpose, and be all [[Well\|well]] borne<br>Without defect. Therefore to France, my liege.<br>Divide your happy England into four,<br>Whereof take you one quarter into France,<br>And you withal shall make all Gallia shake.<br>If we with thrice such powers left at [[Home\|home]]<br>Cannot defend our own doors from the dog,<br>Let us be worried, and our nation lose<br>The name of hardiness and policy. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Call in the messengers sent from the [[Dauphin]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one or more |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Now are we [[Well\|well]] resolved, and by God’s help<br>And yours, the noble sinews of our power,<br>France being ours we’ll bend it to our awe,<br>Or break it all to pieces. Or there we’ll sit,<br>Ruling in large and ample empery<br>O’er France and all her almost kingly dukedoms,<br>Or lay these bones in an unworthy urn,<br>Tombless, with no remembrance over them.<br>Either our [[history]] shall with full mouth<br>Speak freely of our acts, or else our [[Grave\|grave]],<br>Like Turkish mute, shall have a tongueless mouth,<br>Not worshipped with a waxen epitaph. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Ambassadors of France, with a tun |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | Now are we [[Well\|well]] prepared to know the pleasure<br>Of our fair cousin [[Dauphin]], for we hear<br>Your greeting is from him, not from the [[King]]. |
| AMBASSADOR | May’t please your majesty to give us leave<br>Freely to render what we have in charge,<br>Or shall we sparingly show you far off<br>The [[Dauphin]]’s meaning and our embassy? |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | We are no [[Tyrant\|tyrant]], but a Christian [[King\|king]],<br>Unto whose grace our passion is as subject<br>As is our wretches fettered in our prisons.<br>Therefore with frank and with uncurbèd plainness<br>Tell us the [[Dauphin]]’s mind. |
| AMBASSADOR | Thus then in few:<br>Your highness lately sending into France<br>Did claim some certain dukedoms, in the right<br>Of your great predecessor, [[King]] Edward the Third.<br>In answer of which claim, the Prince our master<br>Says that you savour too much of your youth,<br>And bids you be advised, there’s naught in France<br>That can be with a nimble galliard won:<br>You cannot revel into dukedoms there.<br>He therefore sends you, meeter for your spirit,<br>This tun of treasure, and in lieu of this<br>Desires you let the dukedoms that you claim<br>Hear no more of you. This the [[Dauphin]] speaks. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | What treasure, uncle? |
| EXETER | (opening the tun) Tennis balls, my liege. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | We are glad the [[Dauphin]] is so pleasant with us.<br>His present and your pains we thank you for.<br>When we have matched our rackets to these balls,<br>We will in France, by God’s grace, play a set<br>Shall strike his father’s [[Crown\|crown]] into the hazard.<br>Tell him he hath made a match with such a wrangler<br>That all the courts of France will be disturbed<br>With chases. And we understand him [[Well\|well]],<br>How he comes o‘er us with our wilder days,<br>Not measuring what use we made of them.<br>We never valued this poor seat of England,<br>And therefore, living hence, did give ourself<br>To barbarous licence—as ’tis ever common<br>That men are merriest when they are from [[Home\|home]].<br>But tell the [[Dauphin]] I will keep my state,<br>Be like a [[King\|king]], and show my sail of [[Greatness\|greatness]]<br>When I do rouse me in my throne of France.<br>For that have I laid by my majesty<br>And plodded like a man for working days,<br>But I will rise there with so full a glory<br>That I will dazzle all the eyes of France,<br>Yea strike the [[Dauphin]] blind to look on us.<br>And tell the pleasant Prince this mock of his<br>Hath turned his balls to gunstones, and his [[Soul\|soul]]<br>Shall stand sore charged for the wasteful vengeance<br>That shall fly from them—for many a thousand<br>widows<br>Shall this his mock mock out of their dear husbands,<br>Mock mothers from their sons, mock castles down;<br>Ay, some are yet ungotten and [[unborn]]<br>That shall have cause to curse the [[Dauphin]]’s scorn.<br>But this lies all within the will of God,<br>To whom I do appeal, and in whose name<br>Tell you the [[Dauphin]] I am coming on<br>To venge me as I may, and to put forth<br>My rightful hand in a [[Well\|well]]-hallowed cause.<br>So get you hence in peace. And tell the [[Dauphin]]<br>His jest will savour but of shallow wit<br>When thousands weep more than did laugh at it.—<br>Convey them with safe conduct.—Fare you [[Well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Ambassadors |
| EXETER | This was a merry message. |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | We [[Hope\|hope]] to make the sender blush at it.<br>Therefore, my lords, omit no happy hour<br>That may give furth’rance to our expedition;<br>For we have now no thought in us but France,<br>Save those to God, that run before our business.<br>Therefore let our proportions for these wars<br>Be soon collected, and all things thought upon<br>That may with reasonable swiftness add<br>More feathers to our wings; for, God before,<br>We’ll chide this [[Dauphin]] at his father’s door.<br>Therefore let every man now task his thought,<br>That this fair action may on foot be brought. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano [[King|Re]] Harry, i Duchi di [[Gloucester]], [[Clarence]] ed Exeter, e i Conti di Warwick e di Westmorland |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Dov’è il mio grazioso signore di Canterbury? |
| EXETER | Non è qui presente. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Mandate a chiamarlo, buon zio. |
| WESTMORLAND | Facciamo entrare l’ambasciatore, mio sovrano? |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Non ancora, cugino. Vogliamo essere risolti,<br>prima di udirlo, su certe cose di peso<br>che occupano il nostro pensiero, riguardo a noi e alla Francia. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano l’Arcivescovo di Canterbury e il Vescovo di Ely |
| CANTERBURY | Dio e i suoi angeli custodiscano il vostro sacro trono,<br>e a lungo ve ne facciano degno. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Certo, vi ringraziamo.<br>Mio dotto signore, vi preghiamo di procedere,<br>e con giustizia e religione spiegare<br>perché la legge Salica che vigono in Francia<br>debba o non debba sbarrarci nella nostra pretesa.<br>E Dio non voglia, mio caro e fedele signore,<br>che voi foggiate, torciate o pieghiate la vostra lettura,<br>o gravaste con sottigliezza la vostra [[Soul|anima]] consapevole<br>esponendo titoli malnati, il cui diritto<br>non s’accorda nei colori nativi con la [[Truth|verità]];<br>ché Dio sa quanti ora in salute<br>verseranno il loro [[Blood|sangue]] a conferma<br>di ciò a cui la vostra reverenza ci spingerà.<br>Perciò badate a come impegnate la nostra persona,<br>a come destate la nostra [[Sword|spada]] dormiente di guerra;<br>vi ingiungiamo nel nome di Dio: badate.<br>Ché mai due tali regni contesero<br>senza gran caduta di [[Blood|sangue]], le cui gocce innocenti<br>sono ognuna un lutto, un’aspra querela<br>contro colui i cui torti danno filo alle spade<br>che fanno tale strage nella breve [[Mortality|mortalità]].<br>Sotto questo scongiuro parlate, mio signore,<br>ché udiremo, noteremo e crederemo in cuore<br>che quanto dite è nella vostra [[Conscience|coscienza]] lavato<br>puro come il peccato dal battesimo. |
| CANTERBURY | Uditemi dunque, grazioso sovrano, e voi pari<br>che dovete voi stessi, le vostre vite e i vostri servigi<br>a questo trono imperiale. Non v’è ostacolo<br>da opporre alla pretesa di vostra altezza sulla Francia<br>se non questo, che essi traggono da Faramondo:<br>‘In terram Salicam mulieres ne succedant’—<br><br>’Nessuna donna succeda in terra Salica’—<br>e quella ‘terra Salica’ i Francesi ingiustamente chiosano<br>essere il regno di Francia, e Faramondo<br>il fondatore di questa legge e dell’esclusione femminile.<br>Eppure i loro stessi autori fedelmente affermano<br>che la terra Salica è in [[Germany|Germania]],<br>tra i fiumi Saale ed Elba,<br>dove, avendo Carlo Magno soggiogato i Sassoni,<br>vi lasciò indietro e vi stabilì certi Franchi<br>i quali, tenendo a sdegno le donne germaniche<br>per certi disonesti costumi della loro vita,<br>vi stabilirono questa legge: cioè, nessuna femmina<br>fosse erede in terra Salica—<br>e quella Salica, come dissi, tra Elba e Saale,<br>è oggidì chiamata in [[Germany|Germania]] Meissen.<br>Appare dunque [[Well|bene]] che la Legge Salica<br>non fu concepita per il regno di Francia.<br>Né i Francesi possedettero la terra Salica<br>se non quattrocentoventun anni<br>dopo la morte di [[King|Re]] Faramondo,<br>oziosamente supposto il fondatore di questa legge,<br>il quale morì nell’anno della nostra redenzione<br>quattrocentoventisei; e Carlo Magno<br>soggiogò i Sassoni, e insediò i Franchi<br>oltre il [[River|fiume]] Saale, nell’anno<br>ottocentocinque. Inoltre, i loro scrittori dicono,<br>[[King|Re]] Pipino, che depose Childerico,<br>fece, come erede generale—essendo disceso<br>da Blitilde, che era figlia di [[King|Re]] Clotario—<br>pretesa e titolo alla [[Crown|corona]] di Francia.<br>Anche Ugo Capeto—che usurpò la [[Crown|corona]]<br>di Carlo Duca di Lorena, unico erede maschio<br>della vera linea e stirpe di Carlo Magno—<br>per raffinare il suo titolo con qualche parvenza di [[Truth|verità]],<br>benché in pura [[Truth|verità]] fosse corrotto e nullo,<br>si spacciò per erede di Madonna Lingarda,<br>figlia di Carlomanno, che era figlio<br>di Luigi l’Imperatore, e Luigi figlio<br>di Carlo Magno. Anche [[King|Re]] Luigi Nono,<br>che era unico erede dell’[[Usurper|usurpatore]] Capeto,<br>non poté quietarsi nella sua [[Conscience|coscienza]],<br>portando la [[Crown|corona]] di Francia, finché non fu certo<br>che la bella [[Queen Isabel|Regina Isabella]], sua nonna,<br>discendeva per retta linea da Madonna Ermengarda,<br>figlia di Carlo, il predetto Duca di Lorena;<br>per il quale [[Marriage|matrimonio]], la linea di Carlo Magno<br>fu riunita alla [[Crown|corona]] di Francia.<br>Sicché, chiaro come il sole d’[[Summer|estate]],<br>il titolo di [[King|Re]] Pipino e la pretesa di Ugo Capeto,<br>e la soddisfazione di [[King|Re]] Luigi, tutti appaiono<br>reggersi sul diritto e sul titolo della femmina;<br>e così fanno i re di Francia fino a questo giorno,<br>per quanto vogliano innalzare questa Legge Salica<br>per sbarrare a vostra altezza il pretendere dalla femmina,<br>e scelgano piuttosto di nascondersi in una rete<br>che di sbarrare apertamente i loro storti titoli,<br>usurpati a voi e ai vostri progenitori. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Posso con diritto e [[Conscience|coscienza]] avanzare questa pretesa? |
| CANTERBURY | Il peccato sul mio capo, temuto sovrano.<br>Ché nel Libro dei Numeri sta scritto:<br>‘Quando il figlio muore, che l’eredità<br>discenda alla figlia.’ Grazioso signore,<br>fatevi valere per il vostro; spiegate il vostro [[Flag|vessillo]] sanguigno;<br>volgete lo sguardo ai vostri possenti antenati.<br>Andate, mio temuto signore, alla tomba del vostro bisavolo,<br>da cui traete la pretesa; invocate il suo spirito guerriero,<br>e quello del vostro prozio, [[Edward the Black Prince|Edoardo il Principe Nero]],<br>che sul suolo francese recitò una [[Tragedy|tragedia]],<br>facendo scempio di tutta la potenza di Francia,<br>mentre il suo possentissimo padre su una [[Hill|collina]]<br>stava sorridendo a veder il cucciolo del suo [[Lion|leone]]<br>far preda nel [[Blood|sangue]] della nobiltà francese. no<br>O nobili Inglesi, che poterono trattenere<br>con metà delle loro forze tutta la [[Pride|superbia]] di Francia,<br>e lasciar l’altra metà starsene lì a ridere,<br>senza far nulla, e fredda per mancanza d’azione. |
| ELY | Destate la memoria di quei valorosi morti,<br>e col vostro braccio possente rinnovate le loro imprese.<br>Voi siete il loro erede, sedete sul loro trono,<br>il [[Blood|sangue]] e il [[Courage|coraggio]] che li resero famosi<br>scorrono nelle vostre vene—e il mio tre volte possente sovrano<br>è nel mattino stesso di maggio della sua giovinezza,<br>maturo per imprese e poderose gesta. |
| EXETER | I vostri fratelli re e i monarchi della terra<br>tutti s’attendono che vi destiate<br>come fecero i leoni antichi del vostro [[Blood|sangue]]. |
| WESTMORLAND | Sanno che vostra grazia ha ragione; e mezzi e<br>forza,<br>li ha pure vostra altezza. Mai [[King|re]] d’Inghilterra<br>ebbe nobili più ricchi e sudditi più leali,<br>i cui cuori hanno lasciato i loro corpi qui in Inghilterra<br>e stanno attendati nei campi di Francia. |
| CANTERBURY | Oh, che i loro corpi seguano, mio caro sovrano,<br>con [[Blood|sangue]] e [[Sword|spada]] e [[Fire|fuoco]], a conquistare il vostro diritto.<br>In aiuto di che, noi della spiritualità<br>leveremo a vostra altezza una somma sì poderosa<br>quale mai il clero in una sola [[Time|volta]]<br>portò ad alcuno dei vostri antenati. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Non solo dobbiamo armarci per invadere i Francesi,<br>ma disporre le nostre forze a difenderci<br>contro lo Scozzese, che ci piomberà addosso<br>con ogni vantaggio. |
| CANTERBURY | Quelli delle marche, grazioso sovrano,<br>saranno muro bastante a difendere<br>il nostro interno dai predoni di confine. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Non intendiamo i soli ladruncoli scorridori,<br>ma [[Fear|temiamo]] il disegno principale dello Scozzese,<br>che ci fu sempre vicino incostante.<br>Ché leggerete che il mio bisavolo<br>mai scoprì la sua potenza verso la Francia<br>senza che lo Scozzese sul suo regno sguarnito<br>venisse riversandosi come la marea in una breccia<br>con ampia e traboccante pienezza della sua forza<br>tormentando la terra spogliata con caldi assalti,<br>stringendo di grave assedio castelli e città,<br>sicché l’Inghilterra, vuota di difesa,<br>tremò e vacillò al rumore di ciò. |
| CANTERBURY | Fu allora più temuta che danneggiata, mio<br>sovrano. 155<br>Ché udite solo l’esempio ch’ella diede di sé:<br>quando tutta la sua cavalleria era in Francia<br>ed ella vedova in lutto dei suoi nobili,<br>non solo si difese [[Well|bene]] da sé<br>ma prese e chiuse in stalla come bestia randagia<br>il [[King|Re]] di Scozia, che mandò in Francia<br>a colmar la fama di [[King|Re]] Edoardo di re prigionieri<br>e a far la vostra cronaca ricca di lode<br>quanto è la melma e il [[Bottom|fondo]] del mare<br>di relitti affondati e tesori innumerevoli. |
| *(didascalia)* | A LORD |
| CANTERBURY | Ma c’è un detto assai antico e vero:<br>‘Se vuoi la Francia conquistare, dalla Scozia devi cominciare.’<br>Ché una volta che l’aquila Inghilterra è a caccia,<br>al suo nido incustodito la donnola Scozzese<br>viene di soppiatto, e così le succhia le uova regali,<br>facendo il topo in assenza del gatto,<br>a manomettere e sciupare più di quanto possa mangiare. |
| EXETER | Ne segue dunque che il gatto deve restare a [[Home|casa]].<br>Ma questa è necessità forzata,<br>giacché abbiamo serrature a custodire il necessario<br>e graziose trappole a prender i piccoli ladri.<br>Mentre la mano armata combatte all’estero,<br>la testa avveduta si difende a [[Home|casa]].<br>Ché il [[government|governo]], per quanto in alto, in basso e più in basso,<br>diviso in parti, si mantiene in un solo accordo,<br>concordando in una cadenza piena e naturale,<br>come musica. |
| CANTERBURY | Vero. Perciò il cielo divide<br>lo stato dell’uomo in diverse funzioni,<br>ponendo l’operare in moto continuo;<br>e ad esse è fissata, come mira o bersaglio,<br>l’obbedienza. Così infatti operano le [[Bees|api]] del miele,<br>creature che per una regola di [[Nature|natura]] insegnano<br>l’atto dell’ordine a un regno popoloso.<br>Hanno un [[King|re]], e ufficiali d’ogni sorta,<br>dove alcuni come magistrati correggono in [[Home|casa]];<br>altri come mercanti avventurano il commercio all’estero;<br>altri come soldati, armati dei loro pungiglioni,<br>fanno bottino sui vellutati boccioli d’[[Summer|estate]],<br>e tale preda con lieta marcia recano a [[Home|casa]]<br>alla tenda regale del loro imperatore,<br>che, affaccendato nella sua maestà, sorveglia<br>i muratori canterini che costruiscono tetti d’oro,<br>i cittadini civili che stivano il miele,<br>i poveri portatori artigiani che ammassano<br>i loro pesanti carichi alla sua stretta porta,<br>il giudice dall’occhio triste col suo arcigno ronzio<br>consegnando a pallidi carnefici<br>il pigro fuco sbadigliante. Io ne inferisco questo:<br>che molte cose, avendo pieno riferimento<br>a un solo accordo, possono operare per vie contrarie.<br>Come molte frecce, scoccate per diverse vie,<br>volano a un solo bersaglio, come molte strade s’incontrano in una città,<br>come molte fresche correnti s’incontrano in un solo mare salato,<br>come molte linee si chiudono nel centro della meridiana,<br>così mille azioni una volta avviate<br>possono finire in un solo scopo, ed essere tutte [[Well|ben]] condotte<br>senza difetto. Perciò in Francia, mio sovrano.<br>Dividete in quattro la vostra felice Inghilterra,<br>e di essa portate un quarto in Francia,<br>e con ciò farete tremare tutta la Gallia.<br>Se noi con tre volte tali forze lasciate a [[Home|casa]]<br>non sappiamo difendere le nostre porte dal cane,<br>che siamo azzannati, e la nostra nazione perda<br>il nome di ardimento e di accortezza. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Fate entrare i messaggeri mandati dal [[Dauphin|Delfino]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Esce uno o più |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Ora siamo [[Well|ben]] risoluti, e con l’aiuto di Dio<br>e il vostro, nobili nervi della nostra potenza,<br>essendo nostra la Francia la pieghremo al nostro timore,<br>o la spezzeremo tutta in pezzi. O là siederemo,<br>regnando in largo e ampio impero<br>sulla Francia e su tutti i suoi ducati quasi regali,<br>o deporremo queste ossa in un’urna indegna,<br>senza tomba, senza memoria sopra di esse.<br>O la nostra [[history|storia]] a piena bocca<br>parlerà liberamente dei nostri atti, oppure la nostra [[Grave|tomba]],<br>come muto turco, avrà bocca senza lingua,<br>non onorata neppure d’un epitaffio di cera. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano gli Ambasciatori di Francia, con una botte |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Ora siamo [[Well|ben]] preparati a conoscere il piacere<br>del nostro bel cugino [[Dauphin|Delfino]], ché udiamo<br>che il vostro saluto viene da lui, non dal [[King|Re]]. |
| AMBASCIATORE | Piaccia a vostra maestà darci licenza<br>di riferire liberamente ciò che abbiamo in carico,<br>o dovremo con parsimonia mostrarvi da lontano<br>l’intendimento del [[Dauphin|Delfino]] e la nostra ambasciata? |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Non siamo un [[Tyrant|tiranno]], ma un [[King|re]] cristiano,<br>alla cui grazia la nostra passione è soggetta<br>quanto i nostri miserabili incatenati nelle nostre prigioni.<br>Perciò con franca e sfrenata schiettezza<br>diteci la mente del [[Dauphin|Delfino]]. |
| AMBASCIATORE | Dunque, in breve:<br>vostra altezza, mandando di recente in Francia,<br>reclamò certi ducati, in nome del diritto<br>del vostro gran predecessore, [[King|Re]] Edoardo Terzo.<br>In risposta a tale pretesa, il Principe nostro signore<br>dice che sapete troppo della vostra giovinezza,<br>e vi consiglia di considerare che nulla v’è in Francia<br>che si possa vincere con un’agile gagliarda:<br>non potete danzando entrare in possesso di ducati là.<br>Vi manda perciò, più conveniente al vostro spirito,<br>questa botte di tesoro, e in cambio di ciò<br>desidera che lasciate i ducati che pretendete<br>non udir più nulla di voi. Questo dice il [[Dauphin|Delfino]]. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Quale tesoro, zio? |
| EXETER | (aprendo la botte) Palle da tennis, mio sovrano. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | Siamo lieti che il [[Dauphin|Delfino]] sia così faceto con noi.<br>Del suo dono e della vostra fatica vi ringraziamo.<br>Quando avremo accordato le nostre racchette a queste palle,<br>giocheremo in Francia, per grazia di Dio, una partita<br>che scaglierà la [[Crown|corona]] di suo padre nel rischio.<br>Ditegli che ha fatto partita con un tale attaccabrighe<br>che tutte le corti di Francia saranno sconvolte<br>dai rimbalzi. E lo comprendiamo [[Well|bene]],<br>come ci rinfaccia i nostri giorni più sfrenati,<br>senza misurare quale uso ne facemmo.<br>Non demmo mai valore a questo povero seggio d’Inghilterra,<br>e perciò, vivendo lontano di qui, ci abbandonammo<br>a barbara licenza—come è sempre comune<br>che gli uomini sono più allegri quando sono fuori [[Home|casa]].<br>Ma dite al [[Dauphin|Delfino]] che manterrò il mio stato,<br>sarò come un [[King|re]], e spiegherò la vela della mia [[Greatness|grandezza]]<br>quando mi leverò sul mio trono di Francia.<br>Per questo ho deposto la mia maestà<br>e ho arrancato come un uomo dei giorni feriali,<br>ma là mi leverò con sì piena gloria<br>che abbaglierò tutti gli occhi di Francia,<br>sì, accecherò il [[Dauphin|Delfino]] a guardarci.<br>E dite al faceto Principe che questa sua beffa<br>ha mutato le sue palle in palle da cannone, e la sua [[Soul|anima]]<br>starà gravemente accusata per la rovinosa vendetta<br>che da esse volerà—ché molte migliaia di<br>vedove<br>questa sua beffa beffando priverà dei loro cari mariti,<br>beffando madri dei loro figli, beffando giù castelli;<br>sì, alcuni non sono ancora concepiti né [[unborn|nati]]<br>che avranno ragione di maledire lo scherno del [[Dauphin|Delfino]].<br>Ma tutto questo sta nella volontà di Dio,<br>al quale mi appello, e nel cui nome<br>dite voi al [[Dauphin|Delfino]] che io sto venendo<br>a vendicarmi come posso, e a stendere<br>la mia mano legittima in una causa [[Well|ben]] santificata.<br>Andatevene dunque in pace. E dite al [[Dauphin|Delfino]]<br>che il suo motto saprà solo di povero ingegno<br>quando migliaia piangeranno più di quanti ne risero.—<br>Scortateli con salvacondotto.—State [[Well|bene]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Escono gli Ambasciatori |
| EXETER | Questo fu un allegro messaggio. |
| [[King|RE]] HARRY | [[Hope|Speriamo]] di farne arrossire il mittente.<br>Perciò, miei signori, non tralasciate ora propizia<br>che possa dare impulso alla nostra spedizione;<br>ché ora non abbiamo in noi altro pensiero che la Francia,<br>salvo quelli a Dio, che precedono i nostri affari.<br>Perciò le nostre forze per queste guerre<br>siano presto raccolte, e ogni cosa considerata<br>che possa con ragionevole rapidità aggiungere<br>più penne alle nostre ali; ché, con Dio innanzi,<br>rimprovereremo questo [[Dauphin|Delfino]] alla porta di suo padre.<br>Perciò ogni uomo ora impegni il suo pensiero,<br>affinché questa bella impresa sia messa in cammino. |
| *(didascalia)* | Squilli di tromba. Escono |



<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_0" data-title="Henry V — Act II, Scene 0" data-chapter="Scene 0" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/king,motif/fire,motif/horse,motif/mirror,motif/ship,concept/body,concept/fear,concept/greatness,concept/guilt,concept/honour,setting/london,setting/ship,character/chorus,character/henry_v,character/richard"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Chorus]] |
| [[Chorus\|CHORUS]] | Now all the youth of England are on [[Fire\|fire]],<br>And silken dalliance in the wardrobe lies;<br>Now thrive the armourers, and [[Honour\|honour]]’s thought<br>Reigns solely in the breast of every man.<br>They sell the pasture now to buy the [[Horse\|horse]],<br>Following the [[Mirror\|mirror]] of all Christian kings<br>With winged heels, as English Mercuries.<br>For now sits expectation in the air<br>And hides a [[Sword\|sword]] from hilts unto the point<br>With crowns imperial, crowns and coronets,<br>Promised to Harry and his followers.<br>The French, advised by good intelligence<br>Of this most dreadful preparation,<br>Shake in their [[Fear\|fear]], and with pale policy<br>Seek to divert the English purposes.<br>O England!—model to thy inward [[Greatness\|greatness]],<br>Like little [[Body\|body]] with a mighty heart,<br>What mightst thou do, that [[Honour\|honour]] would thee do,<br>Were all thy children kind and natural?<br>But see, thy fault France hath in thee found out:<br>A nest of hollow bosoms, which he fills<br>With treacherous crowns; and three corrupted men—<br>One, [[Richard]], Earl of Cambridge; and the second<br>Henry, Lord Scrope of Masham; and the third<br>Sir Thomas Grey, knight, of Northumberland—<br>Have, for the gilt of France—O [[Guilt\|guilt]] indeed!—<br>Confirmed conspiracy with fearful France;<br>And by their hands this grace of kings must die,<br>If hell and treason hold their promises,<br>Ere he take [[Ship\|ship]] for France, and in Southampton.<br>Linger your patience on, and we’ll digest<br>Th’abuse of distance, force—perforce—a play.<br>The sum is paid, the traitors are agreed,<br>The [[King]] is set from [[London]], and the scene<br>Is now transported, gentles, to Southampton.<br>There is the playhouse now, there must you sit,<br>And thence to France shall we convey you safe,<br>And bring you back, charming the narrow seas<br>To give you gentle pass—for if we may<br>We’ll not offend one stomach with our play.<br>But till the [[King]] come forth, and not till then,<br>Unto Southampton do we shift our scene. Exit |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entra il [[Chorus|Coro]] |
| [[Chorus|CORO]] | Ora tutta la gioventù d’Inghilterra è in [[Fire|fiamme]],<br>e i molli trastulli di seta giacciono nel guardaroba;<br>ora prosperano gli armaioli, e il pensiero dell’[[Honour|onore]]<br>regna solo nel petto d’ogni uomo.<br>Vendono ora il pascolo per comprare il [[Horse|cavallo]],<br>seguendo lo [[Mirror|specchio]] di tutti i re cristiani<br>con talloni alati, come Mercuri inglesi.<br>Ché ora siede l’attesa nell’aria<br>e nasconde una [[Sword|spada]] dall’elsa fino alla punta<br>con corone imperiali, corone e coroncine,<br>promesse ad Harry e ai suoi seguaci.<br>I Francesi, avvertiti da buone spie<br>di questo terribilissimo apparato,<br>tremano nel loro [[Fear|timore]], e con pallida astuzia<br>cercano di sviare i disegni inglesi.<br>O Inghilterra!—modello alla tua interiore [[Greatness|grandezza]],<br>come piccolo [[Body|corpo]] con possente cuore,<br>che potresti fare, che l’[[Honour|onore]] ti farebbe fare,<br>se tutti i tuoi figli fossero buoni e naturali?<br>Ma vedi, la Francia ha scoperto in te il tuo difetto:<br>un nido di petti vuoti, che essa riempie<br>di corone traditrici; e tre uomini corrotti—<br>uno, [[Richard|Riccardo]], Conte di Cambridge; e il secondo<br>Enrico, Lord Scrope di Masham; e il terzo<br>Sir Thomas Grey, cavaliere, del Northumberland—<br>hanno, per l’oro di Francia—oh [[Guilt|colpa]] davvero!—<br>concluso congiura con la Francia paurosa;<br>e per le loro mani questa grazia dei re deve morire,<br>se inferno e tradimento manterranno le loro promesse,<br>prima ch’egli prenda la [[Ship|nave]] per la Francia, e a Southampton.<br>Protraete la vostra pazienza, e noi digeriremo<br>l’abuso della distanza, forzando—per forza—una scena.<br>La somma è pagata, i traditori sono d’accordo,<br>il [[King|Re]] è partito da [[London|Londra]], e la scena<br>ora è trasportata, gentili signori, a Southampton.<br>Là è ora il teatro, là dovete sedere,<br>e di là in Francia vi condurremo salvi,<br>e vi riporteremo indietro, incantando gli stretti mari<br>a darvi dolce passaggio—ché se possiamo<br>non offenderemo alcuno stomaco col nostro spettacolo.<br>Ma finché il [[King|Re]] non venga innanzi, e non prima,<br>a Southampton spostiamo la nostra scena. Esce |



<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_1" data-title="Henry V — Act II, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/king,motif/death,motif/fire,motif/grave,motif/home,motif/sleep,motif/time,motif/well,concept/faith,concept/friendship,character/doll_tearsheet,character/henry_v,character/pistol"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Corporal Nim and Lieutenant Bardolph |
| BARDOLPH | [[Well]] met, Corporal Nim. |
| NIM | Good morrow, Lieutenant Bardolph. |
| BARDOLPH | What, are Ensign [[Pistol]] and you friends yet? |
| NIM | For my part, I care not. I say little, but when [[Time\|time]] shall serve, there shall be smiles—but that shall be as it may. I dare not fight, but I will wink and hold out mine iron. It is a simple one, but what though? It will toast [[cheese]], and it will endure cold, as another man’s [[Sword\|sword]] will—and there’s an end. |
| BARDOLPH | I will bestow a breakfast to make you friends, and we’ll be all three sworn brothers to France. Let’t be so, good Corporal Nim. |
| NIM | [[Faith]], I will live so long as I may, that’s the certain of it, and when I cannot live any longer, I will do as I may. That is my rest, that is the rendezvous of it. |
| BARDOLPH | It is certain, corporal, that he is married to Nell Quickly, and certainly she did you wrong, for you were troth-plight to her. |
| NIM | I cannot tell. Things must be as they may. Men may [[Sleep\|sleep]], and they may have their throats about them at that [[Time\|time]], and some say knives have edges. It must be as it may. Though Patience be a tired mare, yet she will plod. There must be conclusions. [[Well]], I cannot tell. Enter Ensign [[Pistol]] and Hostess Quickly |
| BARDOLPH | Good morrow, Ensign [[Pistol]]. (To Nim) Here comes Ensign [[Pistol]] and his wife. Good Corporal, be patient here. |
| *(didascalia)* | NIM How now, mine host [[Pistol]]? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Base tick, call’st thou me host? Now by Gad’s lugs<br>I swear I scorn the term. Nor shall my Nell keep<br>lodgers. |
| *(didascalia)* | HOSTESS No, by my troth, not long, for we cannot lodge and board a dozen or fourteen gentlewomen that live honestly by the prick of their needles, but it will be thought we keep a bawdy-[[house]] straight. Nim draws his [[Sword\|sword]] O [[Well\|well]]-a-day, Lady! If he be not hewn now, we shall see wilful adultery and murder committed. 36 [[Pistol]] draws his [[Sword\|sword]] |
| BARDOLPH | Good lieutenant, good corporal, offer nothing here. |
| NIM | Pish. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Pish for thee, Iceland dog. Thou prick-eared cur of<br>Iceland. |
| HOSTESS | Good Corporal Nim, show thy valour, and put<br>up your [[Sword\|sword]].<br>They sheathe their swords |
| NIM | Will you shog off? I would have you solus. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | ‘Solus’, egregious dog? O viper vile!<br>The solus in thy most marvellous face,<br>The solus in thy teeth, and in thy throat,<br>And in thy hateful lungs, yea in thy maw pardie—<br>And which is worse, within thy nasty mouth.<br>I do retort the solus in thy bowels,<br>For I can take, and [[Pistol]]’s cock is up,<br>And flashing [[Fire\|fire]] will follow. |
| NIM | I am not Barbason, you cannot conjure me. I have an [[Humour\|humour]] to knock you indifferently [[Well\|well]]. If you grow foul with me, [[Pistol]], I will scour you with my rapier, as I may, in fair terms. If you would walk off, I would prick your guts a little, in good terms, as I may, and that’s the [[Humour\|humour]] of it. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | O braggart vile, and damned furious wight!<br>The [[Grave\|grave]] doth gape and doting [[Death\|death]] is near.<br>Therefore ex-hale.<br>[[Pistol]] and Nim draw their swords |
| BARDOLPH | Hear me, hear me what I say. |
| *(didascalia)* | He draws his [[Sword\|sword]] |
| BARDOLPH | He that strikes the first stroke, I’ll run him up to the<br>hilts, as I am a soldier. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | An oath of mickle might, and fury shall abate. |
| *(didascalia)* | They sheathe their swords |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | (To Nim) Give me thy fist, thy forefoot to me give. 65<br>Thy spirits are most tall. |
| NIM | I will cut thy throat one [[Time\|time]] or other, in fair terms, that is the [[Humour\|humour]] of it. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Couple a gorge,<br>That is the word. I thee defy again.<br>O hound of Crete, think‘st thou my spouse to get?<br>No, to the spital go,<br>And from the powd’ring tub of infamy<br>Fetch forth the lazar kite of Cressid’s kind,<br>[[Doll Tearsheet]] she by name, and her espouse.<br>I have, and I will hold, the quondam Quickly<br>For the only she, and—pauca, there’s enough. Go to. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Boy running |
| BOY | Mine host [[Pistol]], you must come to my master, and you, hostess. He is very sick, and would to bed.—Good Bardolph, put thy face between his sheets, and do the office of a warming-pan.—[[Faith]], he’s very ill. |
| BARDOLPH | Away, you rogue! |
| *(didascalia)* | HOSTESS By my troth, he’ll yield the crow a pudding one of these days. The [[King]] has killed his heart. Good husband, come [[Home\|home]] presently. Exit with Boy |
| BARDOLPE | Come, shall I make you two friends? We must to France together. Why the devil should we keep knives to cut one another’s throats? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Let floods o‘erswell, and fiends for food howl on! |
| NIM | You’ll pay me the eight shillings I won of you at betting? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Base is the slave that pays. |
| NIM | That now I will have. That’s the [[Humour\|humour]] of it. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | As manhood shall compound. Push [[Home\|home]].<br>[[Pistol]] and Nim draw their swords |
| *(didascalia)* | BARDOLPH drawing his [[Sword\|sword]] By this [[Sword\|sword]], he that makes the first thrust, I’ll kill him. By this [[Sword\|sword]], I will. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | [[Sword]] is an oath, and oaths must have their course. |
| *(didascalia)* | He sheathes his [[Sword\|sword]] |
| BARDOLPH | Corporal Nim, an thou wilt be friends, be friends. An thou wilt not, why then be enemies with me too. Prithee, put up. |
| NIM | I shall have my eight shillings? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | A noble shalt thou have, and present pay,<br>And liquor likewise will I give to thee,<br>And [[Friendship\|friendship]] shall combine, and brotherhood.<br>I’ll live by Nim, and Nim shall live by me.<br>Is not this just? For I shall sutler be<br>Unto the camp, and profits will accrue.<br>Give me thy hand. |
| NIM | I shall have my noble? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | In cash, most justly paid. |
| *(didascalia)* | NIM [[Well]] then, that’s the [[Humour\|humour]] of’t. Nim and Bardolph sheathe their swords. Enter Hostess Quickly |
| *(didascalia)* | HOSTESS As ever you come of women, come in quickly to Sir John. Ah, poor heart, he is so shaked of a burning quotidian-tertian, that it is most lamentable to behold. Sweet men, come to him. Exit |
| NIM | The [[King]] hath run bad humours on the knight, that’s the even of it. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Nim, thou hast spoke the right. His heart is fracted and corroborate. |
| NIM | The [[King]] is a good [[King\|king]], but it must be as it may. He passes some humours and careers. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Let us condole the knight—for, lambkins, we will live. Exeunt |
